DIIESS-FASHION-110:IR

LADY DUDLEYS DRESS.

Lady Dudley's dress at the Dublin Horse Show was a perfect dream of daintiness and beauty, says Truth; the whiteness of its white- ness and the lightness of its lightness were most becoming to her dark hair and warm brunet complexion and slight figure. Only think of th, exquisiteness of a costume of softly tucked chips de_ chine and fine Irish lace, with lace sleeves, fichu of mixed lace and chiffon, fastened with diamond brooches, and a large frilly hat to match. On the previous day she had also been in white, a beautiful costume, too, but of more serviceable and less poetic suggestion than the other.

===== 0002601_19050909_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
BLUE SERGE GOWN.

Blue serge promises to be the most popular ot all fabrics this autumn. It is from this useful and becoming material that the gown illustrated in our sketch is constructed. The neat bolero has • deep and closely-stitched yoke to which is gathered the fulness of the bolero, the pleats being drawn into a blue silk waistband. Pointed revers of blue and white striped silk strapped

with plain blue silk turn back from the front of 1 the coat, which fastens with large enamel buttons on a wide stitched band. Gigot sleeves finish in gauntlet cuffs of striped silk. The skirt is plain, close-fitting over the hips, and cut immensely wide about the feet. Its sole adornment consists of a closely-stitched, foot-wide band of material at the bottom. The hat of white felt, sharply turned up at one side, is trimmed with a swath- ing of blue velvet round the low crown and two blue wings and a white velvet rosette under the brim. THE LATE MRS. JOHN 0 PATON

The Presbyterian Women's Missionary, Union of Victoria proposes to raise a memorial to the late Mrs. John G. Paton. The memorial sug- gested is a double one—a church at Onua, Male- kula, New Hebrides, where the Rev. Fred Paton is missionary, and a hospital in Korea. The reason for the former needs no explanation, as Mrs. Paton's life work was in the New Hebrides. In Korea a large field has been allotted to the Presbyterian Church of Victoria, and recently a cry came for an advance into Chin-jo, where there are 750,000 people still unevangelised and uncared for. A Medical mimionary is waiting to go forward into this dark region, and it is de- sired to erect a hospital to the memory of Mrs. Paton, to be called the "Margaret Whitecross Paton Hospital." Mrs. Paton took a warm inte- rest in the Korean work, and was one of its first office - bearers. The amount needed for tlae memorial is £3,000. QUEENS OF BEAUV.',

When one comes to think of it, writes " Am- brosia " in the World, the Englishwoman may not unreasonably claim to be the best-looking woman in the world. For just think of the variety of critics whom we manage to please. Our Japanese allies find us adorable, though gilded and black teeth are the chief. signs of beauty in Madame Chrysantheme. Shahs have written enthusiastically of our lovely women, though in Persia no female would stand any chance who had not a long aquiline nose. Indian princes are always enslaved by English maids; and even the dusky African potentate who was here last year was overthrown by the charms of King Edward's fair subjects. Is it not a fact, too, that all Continental visitors testify to our good looks and our grace, and are only too proud if they can win an English wife? Clearly, then, we must be a pleasing combination of everything that makes woman charming all the world over.

VIYELLA SHIRT BLOUSE.

Our sketch illustrates a neat little morning shirt blouse of white viyella. The yoke is much shaped and but to two sharply-pointed tabs, each adorned by a handsome button. Similar buttons appear on the wide box-pleat which runs from top to bottom of the blouse, being placed on the top of the yoke. Gronus of small tucks appear

on each side of the box-pleat springing from the points of the yoke. Other groups, each con- sisting of seven small tucks, come from under the pointed tabs of the yoke. The small bishop sleeves are finished by cuffs shaped like the yoke and adorned with buttons. A plain turn-down linen collar is worn with this blouse, a finishing touch being given by a cravat of narrow crimson velvet ribbon tied in four loops and two long ends.

MRS. ARTHUR PAGET'S MISFORTUNE.

Mrs. Arthur Paget has decided to submit to another operation, which, it is hoped, will this time effect a final cure. Sir Alfred Fripp has been chosen to perform it in a few weeks. The bones of the fractured thigh have to be united— a difficult and dangerous process. Sir Alfred Fripp has the reputation of being one of the ablest, but also one of the luckiest, members of his profession. He is only just over forty; he has received the honour of knighthood, and has been Surgeon-in-Ordinary to King Edward since 1897.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

GALANTINE OF VEAL —Take the bones and the fillet from 31b. of the best end of a loin of veal, weigh Ph. of the fillet, wrap the remaining piece in slices of fat bacon, and braise it with stock and vegetables for two hours. Scrape the raw veal, and pound it until it is pulpy, add 4oz. of scraped fat bacon and 4oz. of butter, and when it is all nicely blended by pounding work in two eggs and one yolk, season with salt and pepper and a pinch of cayenne. Lay the veal, skin part downwards, on the board, spread it with the farce, then make a layer of scallops of the braised veal, red cooked tongue and truffles, cover all with trhe farce, and put another lager of the garnish, which enclose with more farce, draw it together and sew it with strong thread Wet and butter a napkin, sew the galantine in it its length, secure the ends, and braise it with stock and vegetables in a covered stewpan in cne oven tor two flours, and remove it CO coot in the braise. Take it from the cloth, draw out the thread, wipe the galantine with a napkin, brush it with glaze, and dish with a border of erolltonn of eaßic and an outer border of chopped anic..

CRAT.

The Young Princes in Scotland .—Abergeldie Castle, where the Prince and Princess of Walesare staying with their family, WD.4 for many years the Highland residence of our nresent King and Queen, during the lifetime of Queen Victoria. It is situated shout a couple of miles from Balmoral, at the junction of the rivers Gelder and Dee, but though our late Queen tried on saveral occrs- sloes to purchase it, sae could only obtain a lease of the property from the Gurdon family, to whom it belongs. Of all places, the young Pginces of Wales enjoy their life most at Abergeldie, perhaps because they have more holidays there than elsewhere. The two elder sons of the Prince and Princess have now left, the nursery. and have their own rooms, where they pursue their studies tinder their tutor, Mr. Hansel!, for.nerly tutor to Prince Arthur of Connaught. But they have their holidays like other boys, andanpear to have inherited their groat- grandmother's love of the Highlands, and all things Scottish.. _ . -

&Scottish Wedd in g.—The late 3lr.James Farquharson was itn honoured friend of Qmen Victoria, and the marriage of his dapghter Miss Elo Farquharson with Mr. Evelyn Levesoa-Gower next month, will be of special interest to their Majesties the King and Queen. The Farquharson's at one time owned the greater part of Aberdeenshire, and their Majesties and various mem- bers of the R )yal Fanny were frequeutly the guests of the late Mr. Farquharson at lavercauld. His sou is the present laird and chief of the clan, and there are two sisters, the bride-elect, and Miss Louisa Farquharson. Invercauld, which is situated not far from Balmoral, is a stately mansion, at present lot to Mr. Newmann, the well-known South African millionaire, and be and Mrs. Newmann have been entertaining large house parties on Deeside during the season. Women and Mental Occupation.—Tt has often been remarked how much foolishness may issue from a body of men, when assembled together for a certain purpose, although each individual member may be clever, learned, and distinguished, It is only upon some such hypothesis as this suggests,that it is possible to account for the conclusion of the doctors,recently in conference at Leicester, to the effect that women generally were going to physical ruin and the madbouse,from following mental occupations. Neither men nor women go mad from exercising their brains. A fest may overtax their mental powers but they are the very few, and even they do not go mad. It is the idle and vacant minded people who overcrowd the asylums. The idea that women are deteriorating since they have generally entered upon active careers in all the occupations of life, is as wide of the mark as another which compares unfavorably the modern conditions of life with the so- stalled "good old times." People live longer and healthier lives in spite of all that is said about the "stress of modern life," and the idea that mental work is bad for women seems a singularly out-of-date one. Shopkeepers anl their Customers.—The principal shopkeepers of London and the great provincial cities, appear to be convinced of the wisdom of a policy of in- creased inducements and conveniences for their customers. Several of the great London emporiums will not only send a carriage within a certain radius for an intending purchaser, hut will also allow for a first-claas ticket, free of charge, if a railway journey is necessary. For such clients as those whose purchases take up any length of time for selection free luncheons are always provided, but many of the large establish- ments will now provide a free Finch or tea for any customer whose hill—even if for one item—reedits a certain amount. It may be said that in the long run the public roust ray increased prices for such luxuries, but the difference if any, is not a measurable one, and the policy appears likely to meet with considerable success. Not so long ago, a Parisian shopkeeper went a step further, and announced that the amounts of all customers' bills on a certain unspecified day of the month would he returnable in cost but that savoured too much of the gambling spirit to survive the initial experiment. Coloured lanibrellas.—We are told that a great effort is to be made this winter to make the coloured umbrella fashionable. In the summer we have the parasol with all its gaiety and brightness, but it is argued,how much more we need these enlivening effects in the wet and dismal days, and therefore why not nse the gaily coloured instead of black covered 'Ahrens. So far all attempts to get the public to alter its ways in this respect have failed, and this one seems likely to be no exception to the rule, especially where men are con- cerned. It would be no use unless they could be con- verted, and the average man has an inherent dislike to making himself in any way conspicuous. Even the white top hat seems to have gone out of favour for that reason, though the ging has repeatedly worn one at the summer race meetings and yet it is doubtful even if his Majesty appeared under a beautifully coloured umbrella, whether the reform would catch on with the masculine fraternity. The New Corsets.—Corsets of the old-world type are little worn by eleyantes, who have now reduced this indispensible article of attire to what is practically a hip and waist belt. Those among us who have never tried such a corset will be amazed at the improvement in our figure, and the general comfort it brings about, and it is safe to predict, that having once realised the immense advantage of the hip and waist belt we shall never again be tempted to return to the old-fashioned high erection. An excellent plan is to cut down an old high corset, to a little above the waist, and then bind the cut edge with velvet, or chamois leather. The notion can thus be cheaply experimented upon, and if deemed a success, all high corsets on hand, can be treated in a similar manner._.

A Welcome Innovation.—The blouse of a different colour to the skirt has long been impossible from a modistic point of view, but the notion of one of the leading London firms, of stocking blouses in colours to match its ready-made skirts, is anew and really brilliant one. When selecting a finished skirt, or perchance a ready shaped and trimmed one, a woman has often been filled with apprehension as to whether or not she cold find a blouse, or blouse len ~th to exactly match it, but all this anxiety and trouble may now be considered over as the idea of working the blouse and skirt departments together—so far as colour is concerned—is sure to be followed by all leading emporiums. A Revival.—Coloured necklaces to match the toilette are as modish as ever, but the latest craze is for the short rope of gold, and many of us may be the happy possessors of such treasure. It should promptly tie hunted out, threaded in the form described, and donned -while it may. At the best such a mode must be very short lived, as it is one that will very quickly become all too popular. Wild Edible Vegetables.—Oer tastes in this country do not run mach in the direction of the wild edible plants, to which Earl Brownlovv and others have lately drawn attention. No doubt one reason for this is because it is not generally known what is, and what is not, good to eat. Bracken for instance, says the Earl, is excellent food, and scarcely distinguishable from asparagus when well cooked. Even more is said for the heads of young hops, which when cooked and served up in the same way as asparagus, not only make a delicious vegetable, but also possess valuable medicinal proper- ties. Those who are sufficiently enterprising to experi- ment with new dishes, might try the bracken and the hop shoots, but though nettles, and young thistles, may be as good as spinach, they are not so easy to handle and prepare, and there is also the doubt as to their being of the edible kind. It may be that most excellent vegetables and salads may be culled from hedgerows and thickets, but it is perhaps just as well that it is not the common custom to do so, seeing the danger it would involve of taking that which is not food to eat. MADGE.

TkrErr FROM TIIII MARKET.

At the Warrington Police Court on Monday Alice Appleton, 41, Garabaldi-street, was charged with steal- ing a boy's suit value Gs., the property of Alfred Morton, from a stall in the market.—She pleaded not guilty.—The Chief Constable stated that between 10.30 and 11 o'clock on SAturday night Mr. Morton was closing up his stall in the market when the prisoner was seen by another stallholder. a Mrs. Wilson, to go up to the stall, take the first article she caught hold of, put it under her coat, and walk away Mrs. Wilson at once followed her, and recovered the stolen clothes. Ptisoner at first W 39 allowed to go bat Mr. Morton feeling that it was a public duty that she should be prosecuted she was subsequently arrested by P.C. Burton. She then stated that she was not stealing the clothes, but was taking them to her sister to get 14der opinion about them.—Prisoner who had nothing to say, but who denied any felonious intent, was fined 20s.

SCIENCE Nt)TI-4',S AND GLEANINGS

A NEW MINERAL

Dr. C. V. Burton, of Cambridge, has described in Nature a form of crysta:liscd carbon which he has obtained at comparatively low tempera- tures and pressures by ilsing a molten alloy of lead and calcium, which Is found capable of holding. in solution .s nail quantities of carbon, and to contain on cur:ling the last-named mineral in very minute crystals, which,, like the diamond, belong to Ow cal systom, are trans- parent, and have a very h refractive index. The quantities available have not enabled him to determine satisfactorily the density or hardness, but this, at least. is certain, that he has obtained 'a mineral which is not graphite (the familiar " black.elead," the common form. of crystallised carbon), or cliftonite; the very rare one found by Mr. L. Fletcher, of the British Museum, in a meteorite, so that if not diamond .it must be a new mineral. Professor Moissan, of Paris, Succeeded a few years ago in making small arti- ficial diamonds, hut this was accomplished by melting iron with carbon at the temperature of, the electric arc.

ANIMAL AND PLANT PARTNERSHIPS.

In their search for a definite distinction be.

tween plants and animals biologists at one time fixed on the presence of chlorophyll as a criterion. If this green colouring matter was present the organism was held to be a plant. Then it was found that the green colouring matter of the common fresh-water polype (Hydra Viridis) was due to chlorophyll. And so this substance ceased to be considered an abso- lutely distinctive character of plants. But the researches of Geza Entz and M. Braun shelved that the green colour of the " Hydra " was due to the growth in the tissues of the animal of a one-wiled green alga. A green Amceba and a green Infusorian are also known in which the green colour is due to the growth of algm in the tissues. And all these seem to be cases of part- nership, which is of advantage to both parties. The algre find a convenient place of growth or receive a supply of carbon dioxide from the animal, while. in their turn they liberate oxygen for the use of the animal. They are interesting examples of what Anton de P:ary has called Symbiosis, a living together for mutual advan- tage. At the same time such symbiosis between plant and animal is typical of the mutual de- pendence of the animal and vegetable king- doms. For plants liberate oxygen for the use of animals, while the latter supply carbon dioxide and other plant foods.

===== 0002601_19050909_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
ICE-MAKING BY ELECTRICITY.

What is believed to be the only electrically- operated ice-making plant in the United States, according to tho Scientific American, has been installed at Niagara Falls by the Cataract Ice Company, on the " plate " system. The ma- chinery installed comprises a 100 h.-p. 2,200 volt Westinghouse induction motor to operate an ammonia compressor and smaller motors for the air compressor, brine pump, and crane. The tank, which occupies a space 981 t. long, 151 ft. wide, and 9ft. 9in. high, is divided into eight compartments, each compartment containing four plates. One of these eight compartments is emptied daily, eight cakes .of ice being taken out, each of which latter weighs about 4 tons, or showing a daily capacity of 32 tons. The water, which is supplied from the city mains, is filtered before passing to the freezing compartments of the tank. In the operation of the plant, the anhydrous ammonia is compressed, and thereby heated and converted to a gas. -It then passes to a condenser, where it is brought into contact with cold water, thus reducing it in temperature to the temperature of the water, and conveying it back to a liquid. It is then expanded into the freezing coils, which are encased in the freezing plates. These plates being submerged in the water, the ice forms on the outside, and after a period of eight days a cake of ice has developed or grown. This is acknowledged to be rather a long period for freezing, and experts are trying to lessen it.

===== 0002601_19050909_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
SUBMARINE SIGNALLING.

The system of submarine signalling by means of sound, described by Mr. J. B. Millet at the Institution of Naval Architects, promises to be of great value in navigation. Many experiments have been made with the view of providing sailors with a means of hearing signals sent to them through the sea, or possibly to hear the sound of surf on rocks. Without exception, all the methods devised have depended upon some telephonic electrical apparatus attached to the outside of the ship or lowered overboard into the water. None of these plans has, however, been successful—partly because the noises in She ship itself overpowered the sounds of the signals, and partly because the direction from which the sub- marine signals were coming could not be ascer- tained. The fundamental novelty of the system described by Mr. Millet is that the sides of the ship are used as receivers of the signals. A small tank filled with a dense liquid is attached to each side of the ship. In this a transmitter is placed, and the sound collected is taken by wires to any part of the vessel. If the source of sound is on the port side, tho signal will be detected by the instrument in the tank on the port side; if on the starboard sido, the starboard instru- ment will be affected; if directly ahead, both in- struments will be affected equally. Repeated tests with bells in the open ocean have proved that a bell with a lip several inches thick, and having a high musical note, gives the best carrying sound in water, although such a bell is quite unsatisfactory in the air. Signals sent from bolls of this kind have been detected at a distance of thirteen miles in any weather or sea, even when the ship containing the receiving device was going at full speed. Moreover, a little experience enables the direction of the submarine bell to be located within one-eighth of a compass point, whatever the distance. The new system should be of great service, says Pro- fessor Gregory in a paper in the Leisure Hour, and to seafaring men generally it promises to re- duce the chief dangers of navigation due. to collisions or strandings through fog.

WIRELESS SIGNALS ON TRAINS.

If the experiments which the -.6;;;ag-o. and Alton Railway is now making with a system of wireless telegraph signals prove successful, says a Chicago paper, it wowld seem that they must lead ultimately to a revolutionary change as re- gards safety in the operation of American rail- ways. This road, it is reported, has taken steps to equip all its engines with wireless apparatus. Through this method of communication a train approaching within two miles of another on the same track will make its proximity known auto- matically to the engineer of the ether train, who will have before him an indicator. The ringing of a bell and the flashing of a green light will apprise him that the train is coming from the rear. A red light flashed before him will mean that the train is in front. The ex- change of signals would be simultaneous, both engineers receiving the warnings at the same moment. Should this device prove practicable, its adoption should go far towards diminishing the number of casualties due to collision. Ordin- arily when an engineer has received his orders from the train despatcher, and pulled out on the main line, he is cut off from all chance of com- munication which might inform him as to the movement of other trains.

5,000 DEGREES OF HEAT. The electric arc is one of the most powerful sources of heat that exists, and when the arc is produced on a large scale and confined in a narrow space we have a heat that cannot be sur- passed. The electric furnace is now one of the most marvellous resources of modern science. Here we have electric force transformed directly into heat, and we no longer use heat obtained from chemical combustion as before. The modern electric furnace uses very simple means to obtain its wonderful effects. Two carbon rods, of two or three inches diameter, ~project into a cavity formed in a chalk block. Tho electric are is formed in the centre just over a carbon crucible. A cover of chalk a few inches thick is placed on the top and the arc is en- tirely confined, so that nearly all the heat is kept inside. It is a striking fact that owing to the non-conducting property of the chalk the opera- tor can place his hand upon the top cover, and a piece of ice will remain on it for a long time without melting. It is a striking spectacle to watch the electric furnace when in action. Long flames shoot out from either side through the openings, giving a blinding light accom- panied by a loud hissing noise which the arc produces. The ' operators are obliged to wear glasses which are nearly black, so as to protect their eyes from the intense light while they wat-h the progress of the heatir‘cr. In such a T 7, 300 is reached the extraordinary heat of c.r.rl-907., and at this point nearly everything c: be melted. Even the chalk block fuses on •- inside. A striking experiment is to boil ;,.a. or ordinary sand or flint in a carbon • Not only does the sand melt and boil, given off in the form of vapour. By rerforated cover and placing a bell-jar ,r the furnace for an instant we see the Ye. 'r condense on the inside of the jar in fine der. Almost all known matter is melted I olatilised at such a high heat. In such a 0,„„, A,-,cribecl no less than 150 nurse-power Of electricity is transtormed into heat. [ALL RIG HTS ILES Elt VED . A" STORM

AND ITS CONSEQUENCES,

F. C. SELOITS • (Author of" Travel and Adventure in South East Africa," V.).

Towards the end of May of 1884, a full six weeks after the usual close of the wet season, the most extraordinary rain storm I have ever experienced swept over the desert to the west of Matebeleland. I was at that time travelling slowly westwards by bullock waggon, following no track, but making my way across country nucl,tr native guidance from one pool of water to another.

One afternoon dense masses of black clouds gathered in the west, and presently spread over the whole sky. There was neither thunder nor lightning, but towards evening a strong wind sprang up, and soon afterwards a steady rain began to fall, at first light, but ever increasing in intensity, until soon after dark it was coming down in such a way that I thought it impossible that it could last long. But all through that night, and until midday the follow- ing day, the heayy rain never ceased to fall. During the afternoon, however, the sky again grew lighter, and the rain gradually ceased. By midnight the stars were shining from a cloud- less sky.

AFTER THE DELUGE.

Early the following morning I rode out to see the effect of this unprecedented downpour, and found the face of •the country completely changed. On the sand ridges no difference was apparent, as the thirsty soil had easily absorbed all the rein that had fallen on it, bat the inter- vening spaces where the Mopani trees flourish, and where the soil is a sort of light clay, had been transformed into broad shallow lakes from a few inches to two feet in depth. ,Riding across one of these flooded valleys, I came across a porcupine seated disconsolately on the stem of a fallen Mopani tree—the first of these animals I had ever come across in the 'daytime. The sur- face floods soon soaked away on the level ground, but every hollow became a lake or pond which held water for a longer or shorter time accord- ing to its depth, and when traversing this same tract of cbuntry some five months later, I still found all the larger hollows fairly full, and was therefore able to travel at my leisure with ease and comfort through a cotintry which in ordin- ary seasons would have been quite impassable by bullock waggon at that time of the year.

THE DESERT REFRESHED,

Under these conditions I found this usually arid waste a very pleasant place to wander over. Game, though not very abundant, was still in sufficient numbers to enable me to keep my own people and the several families of Bushmen who had attached themselves to me in rude plenty. Owing to the favourable season all grazing and browsing animals, including my own cattle, were in very good condition, and my larder seldom lacked the choicest portions of the giraffe, eland, gemsbuck and springbuck, four of the best ani- mals for the table, when in prime condition, which South Africa, or any, other part of the world, can produce. Blue wildebeests were more plentiful than all other species of game, and on the broad grassy plains which stretch westwards from Metse butluku—the hitter water—often congregated in herds of from one to two hun- dred individuals.

Here, too, large troops of zebras—Chapman's variety of Burchell's Zebra—were often to be met with, as well as small herds of the Cape hartebeest, now quite a scarce animal, as it has been either exterminated in most parts of its former range, or driven' into the waterless deserts of South Western Africa.

SWARMS WITH GAME-

In the dense thorn jungles which lay a little to the north of my route, a large herd of ele- phants spent the whole year, as I saw their tracks when travelling westwards from Mata- beleland, and again on my return eastwards some five months later. These animals were, however, very wary, never .drinking twice run- ning at one pool, and travelling immense dis- tances every night. I twice followed their spoor for a whole day, and slept on it without coming up with them. But besides this large herd of cow and young bull elephants, there were four im- mense old bulls (judging from their tracks) which frequented the same jungles but lived by themselves apart from the herd. These old Patriarchs I tried hard but unsuccessfully to find in the daytime, and I also watched for them at nights on several occasions at vleys at which they had been in the habit of drinking ; but I never had the luck to hit off the right pool of water on the right night. Once they drank at vley within a mile of the one at which I was watching, and I heard them at the water, but on this occasion I think they must have got my wind, as although I was early on their tracks and followed them all day with the best of Bushmen spoorers, I never got near them, and the next day rode home. shooting a fat giraffe cow on the way.

ELEPHANTS AND LIONS.

I may here remark that it is of little use, if you do not come up with elephants on the first day. to. follow them any fur- ther, as if alarmed they travel very fast and far at nights, and on the morning of the second day will, in all probability be much fur- ther off than they were when you first took up their spoor. Of lions there were a few, but not very many in this part of the country. In one place, where the Bushmen had assured me there were none of these animals about, an old lioness had attacked and bitten one of my horses, though not very severely, and on another occa- sion I had found and taken up the fresh tracks of a lion and lioness, first in sandy ground where it was easy to follow, but later, in the hard, stuabaked soil in which the Mopani tree flour- ishes, and where I eventually lost it. At this time I had long been absent from the tarthest outpost of civilisation, and had not seen a white man's face or spoken a word of English for more than six months. My waggon was then standing beneath some tall feathery-leaved thorn trees near a large vley of water, beyond which stretched a large open plain covered with a rather short growth of yellow grass for South Africa—as it was not more than about two feet in length. This open plain was skirted to the north by dense jungles of wait-a-bit thorns, and on its other three sides by open mopani forest and scrub. My camp was on the northern side of the plain, quite close to the/thorn jungles. I had plenty of books with me to read at nights, and hunting and collecting specimens of natural history filled all my time by day. Ah ! those wore glorious times indeed.

[THE ED.)

WHITE ROSE By GERALD MSS.

CHAPTER IX. THERE was never a very long interval be- tween the entertainments at Hampstead House, which at certain periods of the year always illuminated its corner of Belgrave Square ,with its brightly lighted windows. It was a house famous not only for its mag- nificence but for its hospitality. Two nights after her royal ball, the night after the opening of Parliament and the night of the scene in Trafalgar Square, the Duchess of Hampstead was giving a dinner party which would have been a big one in many house- holds, but was in hers a comparatively small one. She was entertaining her " committee " -as Lady Sabina had 'called it to Ben Nevis, and the duke had quite thrown off his lethargy. As he sat at the head of the table, his eyes sparkled and .he talked with unusual -vigour and interest to the Marchioness of Swanage, who sat on his right. In point of numbers it was a strangely assorted party. Including the host and hostess, it consisted of twenty-five persons, of whom only six were women. The other nineteen were all men of different ages: but a common bond seemed to exist between them. Lady Swanage was second only to the Duchess of Hampstead herself in regal stateliness and social prestige, and was, in her own opinion and everyone else's, a grand dame. In her veins flowed royal blood, the blood of the deposed Stuarts: and of this, though watered down by many generations, she was inordinately proud. Like the Duchess of Hampstead, she alone bowed the knee to royalty; and with her too, it was a constant source of secret grievance. On the other side of the host sat Lady Mary Renfrew, the last representative of the old earldom of Paisley. Had her sex been differ- ent she would have borne one of the proudest titles in the peerage : and her life from its inception had been a source of disappointment to herself and to all her kith and kin. As it was, the earldom was extinct, and she was living out her life to the dregs, as proud as she was poor. Seventy years of age though she was, she was hale and hearty, and peculiarly active for her age. Her heart was bound up in her native country, and she only lived in London the better to disguise her poverty from prying eyes, hoping that it would escape notice in the busy world of town. Her old name was her passport to every house to which she cared to go, but in her pride, despite her poverty, she was very exclusive in her acceptances. The other ladies were Mrs. Stuart Mac- kenzie, Mrs. Mclver, and Mrs. Knox. The first-named was, also, an old lady with grey hair and a stern face. It was thin and expressionless, and looked as though it had been hewn out of rock. But for over half a century she had been the loving and faithful wife of the old Member of Parliament opposite to her. He was an enthusiast in genealogy, and claimed, not without good reason, the title of Strathfoyle, which had been attainted in the rising of 1715, and the estates which had been sequestered at the same time. This, in his knowledge of his due, he had nursed as a grievance all his life, and anyone who would lend him a sympathetic ear on the point was his friend at once. It was the sure road to his heart. In himself he was a most benevolent- looking and not unkindly disposed old gentle- man, absolutely devoted to his elderly wife despite her unprepossessing exterior. Mrs. Mclver was a young forty and made up as thirty to the bewilderment of her oldest friends who knew the truth. She was the wife of Colonel Mclver, of the Scots Guards, and lived only for excitemen# and new sensa- tions. Since her girlhood she had enthusi- astically joined in each new social freak with great gusto, only to wax cold equally quickl7. She was -indeed in temperament the female counterpart of her host, only more volatile ,and less lethargic, lacking also his measure of brains. Her husband was a smart, soldierly man, popular in his regiment and more than Drdinarily ambitious. No one knew him but iked him, but there was at the same time an Impenetrable reserve at the back of his tharacter—impenetrable almost to himselL He was not much given to introspection and was hardly ever really candid to himself. The last of the ladies, Mrs. Knox, was a social enthusiast, who would gladly have bartered her soul for a seat at the Hampstead 'table or a sign of recognition from royaltys She was slightly unbred and too obviously eager in her manner. For years she had schemed to be in the Hampstead set, and it was only of late that she had worked her way into it by joining the committee, introduced by Mr. lan Frazer not without substantial profit to himself. Her husband was a million- aire of the second generation and a weakling. Therefore his friends found his money most aseful, and he was always a great addition to committee or society, when sinews were required for war. On the right of the Duchess of Hampstead tat the Marquess of wanage, a heavy talker Di the old school. His life had in the main men devoted to politics in the Upper House, where he could always be relied upon for a speech, winch was usually sound from a party point of view, if not particularly brilliant. He ' had fondly expected a seat in Lord Fulford's Cabinet, but to his disgust had been passed aver. This fact obviously rankled from the tone of his more recent speeches. He was an example of hard work and unappreciated mediocrity. Opposite to him sat the Earl of Struan, the Secretary of State for Scotland, the head of the Robertson clan, wnich had been almost wiped out at Culloden. He was an enthusiast in matters Scottish, and always at the forefront in all national celebrations. Another member of the Cabinet who was present was Lord Stepney, the President of the Local Govern- ment Board, a dour and unpopular person With a grudge against life in general, but a clever man and a hard worker. Lord Isling- ton, next to him, had also been in the Cabinet, but had proved a failure as First Lord of the Admiralty. He had, therefore, been shelved with a complimentary peerage, and was clever enough to realise it without being generous enough to forgive it. In his heart there was ever a gnawing, but as a rule, especially with his late colleagues, he concealed it under a placid and ever genial exterior. His am- bitions all his life had been purely political, and he would have given his soul for the Premiership. Thus to attaln Cabinet rank and then to be shelved as a failure was gall to him. The others at the table were Lord Branksea, blacker and more sullen than usual in the memory ef his, refusal : the Earl of noted tt)r his sporting proclivities in the bi,7 game world and ambitious for an heiress to prop up the falling fortunes of his house ; the Master of Ciareloch, a captain in the Scots Guaros ; Mr. lan Frazer, exceptionally well satisfied at his surroundings ; Mr. Ellis Gor- don, who claimed the title of Earl of Annan- dale umler a creation by the Old Pretender upou his burlesque accession in 1701 and-often SO called in Jacobite circles, equally elated and talking at his best ; Mr. Reginald Car- ruthers, imperturbable as ever and witty at intervals in hislazy way; Sir Alan Atherley, a tit:tot:war guarar...man with more brain than most of his fellows and a sarcastic tongue ; Mr. William Brinckley, a disappointed parvenu Prepared to pay highly or to go any lengths for social advancement ; Sir Andrew Went- worth, a leading physician, who for some untold reason had fallen out of favour at C:ourt and resented it ; Sir Napier Corclingly, a Scottish baronet of rent'words--nritl hard face ; and, lastly, Mr. Godsmark, a veritable Hebrew Monte Cristo, -ready to spend lavishly if he saw The promise of a qua pro quo. Such were the odd constituents of the tuchess of Hampstead's mysterious com- mittee, which Belsize and his sister regarded "with such an amused lack of curiosity as the latest parental fad. " What did you think of the soccchec in

the House ? " the Duchess ()I Hampstead asked Lord Swanage. . _

_ "A c,ase of the expected happening," replied Marquess. "On those sort of oc,/ncir,ig. yen know, both by instinct and custom exactly what is going to be said, and the un- popularity of a dead man is always glossed over."

If Of course it's etiquette in the House to say nice things about your opponents, as soon as they are safely out of the way," responded his hostess; "but I thought that Mr. Ossory showed more feeling than usual—certainly snore than one would have expected in public. f' He seemed very upset and perturbed about it," broke in the Earl of Struan, "when I talked it over with him. He had a greater personal regard for Sir Henry Mornington than huld have thought possible." . . .

"He may have —been dour," responded the duchess, giving the devil his due; "but he was a tower of strength and the most valuable man in the country on one's side. What a pity he could not see eye to eye with us I " she concluded in a rather cryptic fashion. Lord Struan nodded acquiescence a trifle "Capital article that one of Carruthers," chuckled the Marquess of Swanage, in his heavy fashion. "Quite one of the best political red herrings' I've ever come across. It thoroughly drew that firebrand Rathcon-, nell and diverted the whole discussion.

"Yes, it certainly helped to burke matters when they might have got a little awkward," agreed the lady of the house with a smile. "What is more, I am sure that you would now have the utmost difficulty in persuading a large percentage of the people in the country that the Irish party were not responsible for it. It was a cleverly thrown piece of political Saud, and some is bound to 'stick." "Yes," said Lord Struan, " Rathconnell's impassioned assevera,tions will only help on the qus s'excuse prineiple. , People are thoroughly accustomed to Irish indignation in politics. _

Carruthers, who was a 'little lower down the table, was listening with an amused and lazy smile.

"I'm glad you approve of the ' Morning News' on the subject," he remarked, catching the eye of his hostess. " The paper had to have a theory of its own, like every other paper: so I thought that an onslaught on the Irish would cid as well as anything—especially as it was certain, whether just or unjust," he interpolated, as the butler filled up his glass "to attract attention and annex a certain number of adherents: It is a charac- teristic of Irish nature and methods that they excite violent partisanship—either for or against. This is what makes them such an interesting and useful quantity in politics and journalism. I assure you that my action was purely journalistic in its genesis," he con- cluded gravely. A smile flitted across the faces of his listeners, as the prophet of modern journalism turned his attention to his plate. "Your Phrenix Park' parallel was as lucky as it was unjust," said lan pra,zer from the opposite' of the table, feeling that he had been keeping too mach in the ,background for practical purposes. "The question of its justice was fully thrashed out years ago," .-ejoined Carruthers softly," at the Thnes's ' expense I The thing which pleased me was the paradox of agrarian atrocities in Park Lane." And your partiality for red herrings,' added the duchess, again cryptic. "The good red herring is proverbial," said the editor gravely. "It makes an excellent dish either flor pcliticians or for penny-a- liners."

There was a pause in the conversation which, despite the general tone of banter, seemed to have an undercurrent of gloom beneath the surface. Then spontaneously it broke out afresh in several places at once, and everything came under discussion except politics, which seemed to be suddenly eschewed by common consent. Dinner drew rapidly to a close to make way for the business of the evening. The swift, silent service of the footmen with its ato- matic precision made course follow course without any, appearance of haste, yet without the slightest delay. At length the servants withdrew, and the duke and duchess were left alone with their guests. After a short pause over the port, the duke rose with his glass in his hand. "Ladies and gentlemen," he said, in a voice that struck a note of boyish eagerness, "The King 1" With an elaborate gee ture he passed his glass to and fro across his gold finger.bowl, and then drained it. His guests rose to their feet, raising their glasses and imitating his gesture. "The King," they cried; "the King, God • bless him 1" . It was the revival of an antique custom. "We shall be ready to start in a few minutes," said the duchess, as she went out of the door.

CHAPTER X.

MEANWHILE, careless of CoMMitteeS, Bina and her brother were enjoying themselves away from home. As had been arranged, the former was dining with her aunt, but at the ast moment, with the tranquil disregard of a foung relation, Belsiz 3 had sent a wire, ?leading a more exciting subsequent engage- nent, into which Lady exciting with a goc7tl- - shrug, read a bachelor party. at the ilub and a Bohemian evening to follow. On her way downstairs, Dina looked into :he dining-room to Supervise the decorations 3y force of habit, " White roses," she murmured, admiring, in ipite of herself 'the beautiful bowls down the entre of the long table. " How beautiful ; Dut how ill-omened just now " However, by the time she was driving through the streets in her own particular little electric coupe, the matter had faded entirely from her mind. "How annoying of Charlie to throw me Dyer at the last minute like this," she thought. "I'm sure it will throw Aunt Grizelle's little party out. It will probably only leave Lord Ben Nevis—' Ben' as Charlie calls him—to ook after both of us." And she fell to thinking of the cavalier in question, admitting to herself that he was by way of being an interesting personality. She found her aunt alone in the drawing- room. "That naughty, annoying Charlie ! " ex- claimed Lady -Grizelle, as she kissed Bina lovingly; " he has put me in the cart again, as he would say. The wild oats season has now commenced, and elderly aunts are at a discount and sisters, too," she added with an indulgent laugh. " However, I was lucky enough to get a little part:, carve after all. Sir Hugo Verschoyle happened to be calling just as Charlie's wire arrived, and I told him frankly how matters stood and persuaded hi a to take an evening off. He's frightfully worried just now." "I expect he is," said Lady Sabina gravely. " It's enough to make him." • The door opened, and Ben Nevis walked in. "Here you are," said Lady Grizelle, greet- ing him cordially ; "we are so glad to see you. Charlie with the contempt born of familiarity has jilted us for something more lively: but I've been lucky enough, to secure Sir Hug Verschoyle at the last minute to fill his place. So we shan't miss him," she concluded with a touch of viciousness. "Relatives don.'t as a rule run to manners," acquiesced Ben Nevis, as he shook hands with Bina, looking up at her with a frank smile ; and Master Charlie is very young yet." Sir Hugo Verschoyle entered. " I hope I'm not late," he •began in stereotyped fashion. " Not a bit," answered Lady Grizelle, nip- ping his unnecessary apology in the bud "we're early. We are going to see the new piece at Wyndham's, and .it begins at 8.30. Do you know Lord Ben Nevis ? " The two men shook hands. "I was just saying that we could do with. out Belsize, now that we have got you instead, Sir Hugo," went on the aunt of the faithlesE guest. "Will you take me down to dinner? At dinner, however, the conversation was general. "So the King has returned to Windsor ? " said Lady Grizelle "Yes," replied Sir Hugo ; "he went this afternoon. As we are responsible for his -efety, we usually znow Ins movements eve* better than he does himself. But in these Loyal times the responsibility has resolve. itself rather into a red-tape sinecure." _

(' It's a very prosaic age," said Bina. "with & horrible amount of monotony about it The worst of life nowadays is that it is so cut and dried by conventions that it is practically the same this year, last year, or next year, until you almost forget which it is." c' I should call this year, for otfce in a way, rather sensational," contended Ben Nevis, but, of course, as a newly returned exile, I suppose I'm no judge." "Oh, I don't mean murders and those horrid things. Thank goodness they don't redly enter into iour lives. By the way, aunt," she went on, "what do you think of Henderson's taste and tact? Henderson is our old head gardener at Southsea Castle," she explained, in parenthesis. • "He has actually sent up white roses for the deco- ration of the table to-night, for mamma's committee dinner. She will be annoyed when she sees it ! "

" Gardeners will be gardeners," mur- mured her aunt, "but it is certainly a most unfortunate selection 'this year. He pro- bably never reads the papers, and looked upon it as the most appropriate decoration for this Jacobite anniversary. Did you see the gay statue of King Charles die First to- day, Lord Ben Nevis?" "No, I haven't been near Trafalgat Square to-day," he replied: "and, what is more, I must own in this prosaic age to having no very quixotic feelings of loyalty to the Stuarts, though it would make my worthy ancestors squirm in their coffins to hear me say so. i They would not accept the inevita- ble in a philosophic spirit and lent a hand to a lost cause. The consequence was that the family estates were sequestered owing to the '45 rising: and since then their descendants have had to live from hand to mouth. Personally, I bear a grudge against the Stuarts for my present parlous state of poverty," he con- cluded, smiling. "Oh, you'll have to marry an American heiress, I'm afraid," said *Lady "Grizelle. You ought to have a. difficulty, as they will always pay a little ex 'a for a romantic piece of history. What a lucky thing the title was not attain', dat the same time I "

Ben Nevis laugh "The modern barter of peerage for peli does not appeal to me, I fear.' Sir Hugo Verschoyle had been sitting very quiet, thinking abstractedly. Suddenly he seemed to awaken.

"That wreath business is very absurd," he said, "and ought to have been put a stop to long ago. However, it's a harmless fad, and it would be difficult to do it without giving offence to some very big people. Besides, it has grown into a regular dustom now, and even in thesT democratic days it's very hard to uproot customs in this naturally con- servative country." . . .

"Yes, you Mustn't take these things too seriously in your official capacity, my dear Sir Hugo," remarked Lady Grizelle. " For my part, I like to see the peerage at play. At.: a rule they are too bored nowadays even to play, or to show any interest when they do. It's a harmless little outlet for their pent-up feelings." "Poor mamma," said Bina, smiling, "it would hurt her feelings to hear 'you talk like this. There is nobody more loyal, but it is quite her ng,west fad. I believe she feels she owes it to Yer Scottish ancestry." They all laughed, including Sir Hugo. "That the Duchess of Hampstead has lent herself to this petty piece of unconscious disloyalty gives it a cachet and disarms my carping criticism," he said, dismissing the subject. _ .

Once or twice during dinner the ques- tion of the sensation of the hour cropped up, but Lady Grizelle firmly tabooed it. I won't have you talk shop in front of poor Sit Hugo," she said decisively, addressing herself to Bina and Ben Nevis jointly. "Let the poor man have his dinner in peace and forget his position as our premier policeman." Sir Hugo laughed. . . . .

" That expresses my position in a nutshell, and I can assure you that though full of interest at times it has its drawbacks and its sordid side."

" The interest of it lies in the solution of problems. I suppose ? " asked Ben Nevis, interestedly. "Yes, in the scientific aspect of criminology. and the straightforward deductions to be drawn from facts," was the answer. "Nat- urally, there is a lot of routine work, of general superintendence and of organisation, which are very necessary, it somewhat hum- drum. But the science of detection itself is most absorbing. Each case presents a fresh* problem. You have before you certain data you have to sift the wheat from the chaff, and draw correct deduction. To do this properly," he continued, warming ,to his tubject, "a man needs an immense amount Df general knowledge, and wants to be thoroughly acquainted with all sorts and conditions of subjects. In the first place, he requires considerable scientific knowledge, or else he will at the very outset be tripped up by the simplest and most blatant fact. Of every subject indeed, it may be said, not one scrap of knowledge can be superfluous. However absurd or useless things niay appear, you can never tell when they may not assist you in a train of reasoning, or to a clue just at the criticll moment."

" Do' you think that the science ot detection has made made much progress of late years?" asked Lady Grizelle. _ . . . .

" Incalculable," repl;en Sir Hugo,* de. cisively. "The French as a nation were always ahead of us, and at one time the British were woefully behind them. But during the last thirty or forty years we have been making up their leeway rapidly. The science of detection owes a great deal to Canler, Claude, Mace, Goron, and others, of whom Mace was probably the most' brilliant reasoner. Then take Bertillon and his system af measurements, which has simplified the question of identification so wonderfully. And in England take Galion with his system of finger prints, elaborated by Henry, and take the various valuable books on medical jurisprudence. That is speaking only of facts, irrespective of fiction. But I am allowing myself to monopolise the conver- sation,' he concluded, apologetically. Lady Grizelle took no notice of his last sen- tence, " What about fiction'?" she asked.

"Take Edgar Allen Poe, for instame," went on Sir Hugo. "He probably did more to help the ordinary indiviqual to apply close reasoning to the facts before him than anyone else. His detective stories and his other examples of logical deduction are the most brilliant in the language. Gaborian France solved his mysteries by pure logic, often in a very instructive way; and then in England came Conan Doyle, who, though by no means original or free from the stigma of plagiarism, did a great deal to popularise Poe's methods. He founded regular school of such stories and helped in real life to make people more or less uncon- sciously deductive in their minds. But truth is stranger and more remarkable than fiction in these matters, as well as others. No Dupin or Holmes ever reasoned a Case more brilliantly than Mace. Take the Voirbo case as an example—a masterpiece and nothing else. But half century ago and more Poe was a pioneer and almost unappreciated: and in real life, in England, Inspector Whycher who solved the famous 'Road Mystery, by successive steps of logical reasoning and successive steps of common sense, was simply laughed out of the force for his pains.l Sir Hugo stopped sharply.

The Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee have resolved," That the Borough Surveyor be requested to report upon the total cost of the laying out and fitting up of the bowling green at Victoria Park." The receipts from the green for two weeks were £7 3s. Bd. The minutes were carried.

TRAMWAY IMPROVEMENTS.

The Electricity and Trimways Committee recom- mended that the tender of Mes3l s. Johnson and Phillips, of London. rot the supply of cables at £531 be accepted, and that the tender of Hadfield's, Limited, of Shedield, for the supply of junction and carves for nylands-street at £614 9s. be accepted. The adoption of the minutes was moved by Alderman SAI ETU riasT, seconded by Councillor WILK t NSON. Councillor EVANS asked for information with regard to the tender for the supply of junction and curves. Alderman TINNION explained that the amount was the cost of the rails only, for the alteration at Rylands- street by carrying the permanent way a certain distance up Bridge-street. Paving, &c., would be extra. Alderman SALETIICRST said the committee believed the improvement would result in a saving, for although the cost. of the equipment would he £l,OOO, it would do away with a large amount of the wear and tear on the curve. With regard to the parchase of cables, a corres- pondent in one of the local newspapers had asked why the tender was not given to ft War.ington firm. The Committee had great reluctance in sending the work out of town, but there were so many prople watching the actions of the Council, and it must be the rule that the lowevt tender should be accepted. The St. Helens Cable Company's tender was £55 more than that of Messrs. Johnson and Phillips. The cables would be of entirely English make. The minutes were approved.

THE PURCHASE OF QUICK-HITCHING HARNESS. "A RIDICULOUS PURCHASE." "TO KEEP THE MUMMY COMPANY."

At a meeting of the Fire Engine Committee the Town Clerk reported that the resolution with relation to the acceptance of the tender of Mr. George Venn for quiet- hitching harness at the price of £lB 10s. had not been confirmed by the Council whereupon it was explained that the Borough Surveyor, acting! on the assumption that the purchase of the harness was urgent and the tender having been accepted by the committee had given the order to Mr. Venn, and that the harness had been delivered. Resolved, "That the Council be requested to confirm the same." Alderman SMETHURST moved the adoption of the minutes. He had had nothing to do with the purchase as he was not present at the meeting of the committee when this course was decided upon. Councillor PEACOCK seconded, lie remarked that the tenders for the self-hitching harness had been accepted in the Chairman's absence. Alderman Smethurst was present when tenders were asked to be advertised for, and these tenders were opened at a subsequent meeting and the lowest accepted. The committee did not give instructions for the harness to be bought at once. The person who had ordered it— prob lbly the Borough Surveyor—evidently thought the purchase an important one and that it should be made lat once, and never expected that any exception would be taken to it. The harness would fit any ordinary horse, and it was as well for them to be as up-to-date as possible.

THE PILGRIM'S SONG.

Ali yes, both long and dreary. TLe" little waile " appears ; And, sometimes faint and weary, We yield to doubts and fears. Tbe road becomes more lonely As friends grow less each day ; For kindred spirits only Can commune by the way.

Then there are times of sorrow Which pilgrims only know; And burdens for the morrow Will bow the spirit low. But mixed with all the sadness, The weariness and pain; There are some drops of gladness, Some cordials to sustain. How sweet Salvation's story, What balm in JeStlef love! And hope of coming glory Will lift the soul above! Ere long with kindred sleeping, In shAdes tho Saviour blest; Safe in Ells gracious keeping, These weary frames shall rest. That sleep shall know no waking, Through dreams or pun or care; Nor strife, nor tumalt breaking, Disturb the silence there. But not, dear Lord, for ever Shall this our portion be ; The grave can only sever A little while" from Thee. Soon through its chambers holy, Through all creation round ; To call the meek and lowly, The trump of God shall sound. Then all Thy likeness wearing, The quick and dead shall rice ; And, in Thy glory sharing, Shall meet Thee in the skies. J. H. W. CLINDINNING.

THE signature of the RUSSIAN POLICY. Peace Treaty is

coincident with an interesting discussion in S. Petersburg between the partisans of Russia's traditional policy and the men who think its day is over. In London and Paris there were congratulations over an article in the "Russ," renouncing all those Asiatic adven- tures which have occupied the Russian Foreign Office for so many years. "Let's have no more talk about revenge on Japan," said the "Russ." "The thing is impossible. Wo can never have a navy to cope with the British and Japanese navies. Let us give up this idea about threatening India. England and Japan combined make that absurd. Let us remember that we are a European Power, and that in Europe our interests and England's interests do not conflict."

This is a new note, and it is perfectly true. Russia's interest in Europe is concerned with maintaining the equilibrium of power, largely for the benefit of her French ally. With this aim England is in perfect accord ; it is the KUSER that objects. He woald never have made a dis- turbance in Morocco but for Russia's temporary weakness. The only Power that cannot relish the new turn of events is, says the " Russ;' the Power that has been "conniving at Russia's effacement." She will now become once more one of the "decisive factors " of European policy, and she will have no further quarrel either

with England or Japan. The Anglo-Japanese Alliance secures the domination of England in Southern Asia, and Russia must make the best of it." Naturally, these views can excite no hostility in this country. It suits us perfectly well to have the status quo assured, and to be relieved from all anxiety on the score of Russian designs. But the thoroughness of the renunciation,as it is preached by the" Russ," may excite a little suspicion. The process of conversion is, perhaps, a little hasty. The individual may have a sudden inspiration of grace which reforms him altogether ; but there are no precedents for such a change in the history of States, especially States which are governed by autocrats.

It is probable enough that the policy of Russia will be greatly modified by the new supremacy of Japan in the Far East, and by the Japanese com- pact with Great Britain. It is said at Sc. Peters- burg, moreover, that the views expressed by the " Russ •' ara undoubtedly entertained by the best and most competent minds in the country." Unfortunately, there has never been any reason to believe that their minds exercise much influence with the Czta. There is serious ground for the suspicion that the rejection of the indemnity was believed in the Imperial circle to make the continuance of the war inevitable. The CZAR had been persuaded that General LINEEVITCH was really about to defeat the Japanese; and he never imagined that Japan would give way on the crucial point in the Portsmouth negotiations. Of the total demoralisation of Linievitch's any we get a vivid glimpse in the article of a Freneh correspondent in Manchuria, whose studies of Russian organisation convinced him before the battle of Mukden that for Russia the war was hopeless. But the CZAR remained in perfect ignor. ance of the military situation from first to last. There can be little hope that a mind so constituted. and so influenced is capable of grasping the lessons, military and political, which, as the really enlightened observers in St. Petersburg perceives "must sooner or later come home." To the CZAR they may never come home. He may always be a prey to intriguers who will toll him a that the Persian question, for example, affords Russia an oppor- tunity of 'baffling England. He will always be urged to profit by some alleged opportunity to recover the Asiatic prestige which the victory of Japan and her alliance with England have cost him. It is easy to say that Russia must abandon the policy of adventure in Asia ; but how are the people, Grand Dukes and others, who have lived by that adventure to employ their energies? The question is not, perhaps, of immediate importance but it will come up again before long.

By an application to 1 THE HONOUR OF THE the Attorney. General for CouNcrt. leave to prosecute two of its members, under the Corrupt Practices Act, the Warrington Town Council is takino, praiseworthy ateps to vindicate itself. The twO'Councillors concerned are both smarting under very serious charges affecting their honour as public men, and in their interests as much as in the interests of :good government and municipal purity, it is necessary that a proper course should be taken to sift matters to the bottom. Not very long ago, we believe, anything in the nature of a public prosecution by the Cor- poration of two members of the Council would have been a very improbable event, but there has been a very welcome and necessary quickening of public spirit in the town during the past few years. We hope the Attorney-General will prove disposed to allow the law to take its natural course in what are likely to prove local causes celebres.

The town is, we SECRECY AND susPxcion. think, indebted to . .

Alderman Pierpoint for his straightforward speech and solento warning at the Council meeting on Tuesday against that secretiveness which engenders suspicion. There is no doubt that the lack of information in regard 1, to the large expenditure on the waterworks !extensions has had a great deal to do with the distrust in which the Water Committee is held in certain quarters. It appears to us that as a 30131 naittee the members have failed to realise their responsibilities, or we should never have had the muddle into which the business of the committee ' appears to have sunk. A conspcuous feature of the numerous debates—heated though many of them were—has been the uniform silence of the members of the Water Committee when the huge contract for which they are responsible has 1:1(.!en discussed. The conclusion has been unavoidable that they knew very little about the matter and were unable to offer any assistance in straightening out the tangle which has proved so vexatious. Even now the Council is unable to get to know to what extent the town is involved or how f.r the known excessive ex- penditure is attributable to a surplus of water and extra pumping. This (Friday) evening :the whole Council is to meet as a General Purposes Committee in order to examine the documents relating to the Waterworks Contract. We have little hope that they will be any wiser in con- sequence, seing that the multitudinous papers weigh about one hundredweight and a half. The only possible way of getting to know exactly how matters, stand is, we think,by adopting Councillor Pemberton's suggestion and appointing a com- mittee of investigation. It is incumbent upon the council to take steps which will allay the very widespread public dissatisfaction with the business of the Water Committee which ur.- doubtedly exists.

It is not with the MARKET RIGHTS. intention of offen-

sively interfering with Cheshire farrnerslwho have sold potatoes at Stockton Heath for half a century that the Cor- poration of Warrington comtemplates taking action, out to preserve its valuable market rights which recent events have seriously menaced. We do not overlook the fact that what is known as the Stockton Heath potato market haa never been looked upon with favour in Warrington, but so long as it maintained its casual character not much official notice was taken. Now, however, the farmers have forsaken their stand on the high road and availed themselves of a convelient field for tha sale of their produce. Herein we have the thin end of the wedge. What appears merely natural self-defence by the tarmers may develop into something which the Corporation of Warrington is obliged to take , cognisance of. The establishment of a fixed ' market so near as Stockton Heath could not be winked at however favourably inclined to the farmers the Corporation might be. Warrington's market rights are a valuable asset to the rate- payers and must be maintained. We hope that no legal action may be necessary. Recognition, in the shape of a nominal toll, that Warrington holds the prior right, would probably appease the wrath of the Corporation and would be no hardship to the farmers. They have, we think, everything to gain by the adoption of pacific measures.

The proposal of the A. LIVING WAGE. two Labour mem-

_ -- hers that the Council should fix a minimum wage of 245. for all able-bodied men in their employ touches the fringe of a very wide question. What is a living wage ? Councillor Stott puts it at 24s.—hence the proposed minimum. We should be very glai to see the Corporation in the role of a model employer, but under present conditions we have little sympathy with the motion before the Council on Tuesday. Very much greater consideration is required before a number of men are raised from 17s. or 189. to 245. per weck for sentimental reasons. Take the scavengers, for instance I They are ado-

quately paid at 17s. considering their standard of physical fitness and the work they have to (b. Would a 245. minimum be even fair or beneficial in their case? Not at all. The immediate result would be a sudden influx of younger and smarter men all eager to get a good wage for an easy job. Individually the" living wage" would thus be a death-blow to many of the present workmen. This is perhaps narrowing:the issue somewhat unfairly, but it is an aspect of the case which is bound to be considered. There are weightier reasons for rejecting the Labour men's somewhat revolutionary proposal, and they are influenced by local trade conditions, local rating and the like. It would cost the ratepayers over £2,000 to put less than 300 Corporation employees on a 245. wage. That is another fact which cannot be overlooked. From a practical workable point of view we think Messrs. Stott and Wright made out rather a poor case for "a living wage," except as a Socialistic sentiment.

We understand that PROPOSED JUNIOR , there is every pros- LIBERAL ASSOCIATION. pect of a new Liberal

organization being formed in Warrington. The object—a very com- mendable one—is to enlist more directly than hitherto the services of the younger supporters Of the progressive cause. A number of ardent spirits have had the project under consideration for some time, and a preliminary meeting has been held this week. At another gathering to he arranged at an early date the formation of the Association will be definitely proposed, and other details discussed. There is ample material in Warrington to build up a healthy junior organization, and the accession of strength to the parent association would be considerable. To secure the active interest of young men in the political affairs of the day is an aim worthy of every encouragement. The great issues before the country to-day demand the thoughtful attection and united action of all Liberals—young and experienced aHre —and if the junior Association is successfully launched we believe that it will soon become a power in the town.

As affording some WARRINGT3N'S Mr:sleet, insight into War* TASTE. rington's musical taste the annual report on the past season's work of the Warring- ton Musical Society is particularly interesting. Briefly stated, the Society has found that the production of classical works is not successful from a financial standpoint. The loss on four concerts amounted to £4B and there is, it must toe regretfully admitted, justification for the con- clusion of the hon. secretary (Mr. W. E. Atkin) that "the appetite of the public for music of a class wrathy of such a society es the Warrington Musical Society is appar- ently generally feeble." Warrington is by no moans alone in this respect, and it would appear that eflorts to educate the mass of the people to an appreciation of oratorio and the higher kinds of music are to a large extent unavailing. While persevering nobly in its eda- cational work, the Warrington Musical Society has latterly realized that it c•sn also serve a useful purposs, and at the same time place itself on a soander financial footing, by arranging excellent productions of light opera. Unquestionably the general public has been delighted with the depar- ture and the society no longer ta moans an adverse, balance but has a substantial sum in hand. The prospects for the future are bright and if the public will accord to the concerts that measure of support which they certainly deserve, the success of the society will indeed be complete.

SOCIETY.

Annual Meeting.

I

The members of the Warrington Photographic Society' held their annual meeting at the Old Academy 00 Tuesday evening, there being a good attendance. In presenting his annual report, Mr. SMITITSOS, energetic hon. secretary, said he did so with feelings c". satisfaction inasmuch as during the past year the objects for which a society like theirs existed had been achieved. The committee desired to place on record their gratitude to the President for his continued Interest in the Society. During the winter fourteen meetings, comprising demonstrations and lectures, bad been held, each of which had been well-attended. The social side had been well sustained by a ce''' cert party under the direction of Mr. Henri Bond. The summer outings were restricted to three and were very well attended. Two were, by the courtesy' of Messrs. J. White, Mclver and Company, to local beauty spots and one was to Delarnere Forest. committee hoped that a permanent moment° of the latlel outing, in the shape of an enlarged photograph, vroulo shortly adorn the walls of the reading room. TbS. membership of the society had been maintained an stood at about 80. The committee however desired to point out that every member of the society must be prepared to do his or her best to add to that number by the introduction of working members. The annual exhibition was in every respect—artistically and fiasfl cially—a great success, and the committee desired to express their thanks to those who had contributed to that success particularly to Mr. Frank Knot for his design of the placque, the Rev. R. Rendell, Dr. C. Thurston Holland and James Shaw for their invaluable services as lecturers, and Messrs. Robert Pierpoint, M.P., J. P. Croft, A. Marlow, and Dr.Tharston Holland for their pictures and to Mr. C. Madeley for the assistance rendered bY him before and during the exhibition. The syllabus_ for the coming season was not completed but it Nos a particularly pod one and included lectures by Messrs. A. H. Hughes, Llandudno ; C. J. Harrison,Manchester..l J. P. Croft, Birmingham; H. Wade, Manchester; Couthurst, Manchester ; J. Shaw Manchester others. The committee hoped shortly to make certa!rt alterations for the convenience of members using la: dark room and also to provide new apparatus. RI C 00 elusion the committee expressed their thanks 0 the ._,O local and photographic press for their reports of proceedings of the Society. The balance sheet which was placed before the meet- ing showeda balance in hand of about £2.ted- The report and statement of accounts were adop During the past year the Society has been admirrakneli served by its various officers and the members 11:11 the their appreciation of the .excellent conduct business of the organization, by re-electing them. u"y.. are as follows :—Mr. F. V. L. Mathias, president; Alderman Bennett, Mrs. Mathias, and Messrs. Fairhurst, E. J. Hall, J. Harding, NV. Winstanley, Taylor and P. Dalton, vice-presidents: F. Galleo; treasurer ; A. C. Smithson, secretary; F. J. Fl.l curator; H. Warburton, lanternist; J. V. " 7,0. e, C. assistant lantern ist P. Dalton, auditor; and Mrs. A. Smithson and Messrs. D. S. Burrell, K. F. Bishop, A. The Warrington " Examiner " is now published on Friday afternoon, price one penny, and is on sale at the various newsagents at about 4 o'clock. Should any difficulty be experienced in getting the paper the proprietors will undertake to deliver a copy at any address in Warrington by 5 o'clock on Friday after- noon without extra charge. Warrington P.S.A. An excellent meeting was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street on Sunday, when Mr. P. Green occupied the chair. Miss Lucy Evans sang with great effect "Never lose sight of Jesus," and in this she was assisted by Master W. Martin. She also sang "0 dry those tears." Mr. B. Wadsworth was the accom- panist. Mr. E. W. Jones gave a capital address, his subject being "What think ye of Christ?"

Emmanuel Church Choir. The annual picnic in connection with the above took place on Saturday last to Southport. The party num- bering over forty was accompanied by a few members of the congregation, and left Warrington by the 1,27 p.m. train, Southport being reached in good time. After several places of amusement had been visited, tea was provided at the Baths Cafe. The remainder of the day was spent in boating on the Marine Lake, char-a-banc and motor rides. A start being made for home at 9.5 p.m. Warrington was reached shortly after 10 o'clock. everybody being thoroughly pleased with their day's outing.

Marriage at the Parish Church. There was a pretty wedding at the Parish Church, on Tuesday, the contracting:Tparties being Miss Adelaide Kate Racey, daughter of Mr. Edwin Racey, Parr-street, and Mr. James William Beddall, Hood- lane, Sankey. The officiating clergyman was the Rev. G. A. Dearden. The bride was becomingly attired in a dress of fawn cloth trimmed with silk medallions, with hat to match, and carried a bouquet of choice flowers. She was attended by three bridesmaids: Miss Charlotte Humphreys and the Misses Hilda and Mona Beddall. Mr. J. Hatton was the hest man. The honeymoon is being spent at Newbridge, Cheshire.

Mr J. K. Broadhurst.

The "Wallasey News" gays the many friends of Mr. J. K. Broadhnrst, the chief clerk at Birkenhead Post Office, will be pleased to learn that he has been appointed postmaster of Ashton-under-Lyne, and will probably take over his new duties on the 25th of this month. Mr. Broadhurst entered the service at War- rington, being appointed a sorting clerk tn 1873. By a strict attention to his duties and general ability he gained the respect of his superiors, and was appointed ' chief clerk at Warrington in 1886,his promotion being a N ery rapid one. After 15 years service in that position he was promoted chief clerk at Birkenhead in 1901, and has remained here until the present time. ISince his appr,intment here he has been the means of many little changes for the benefit of the staff, and it ,is safe to say that Birkenhead's loss is Ashton-under- Lyne's gain. TI(ETLOCK. BRIDGE-STREET.—Advt. Shepherds' Presentation. The quarterly meeting of the Olive Branch Lodge of the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds was held on Saturday evening at the Rose and Crown Inn, Bridge street, when the chair was occupied by the worthy master, Mr. J. Edwardson supported by the deputy master Mr. William Miles. The quarterly accounts were presented by the secretary, Mr. J. Haley, and showed a substantial gain upon the quarter's Workings Several improvements in the working of the lodge, which were m the members' favour,were heartily com- mended and approved by the large ghthering of mem- bers present. At the conclusion of the lodge business a very pleasant ceremony took place. The Distriet , Officers being present, the worthy master called , upon the District Chief to present to Bro. Miles a splendidly framed emblem of merit, for services rendered by him to the Lodge, during the long period of 30 years connection therewith. Bro. Mather in making the presentation said it afforded him the very greatest pleasure to do so, because he believed that this recognition of services was well merited, and he had no doubt would be highly appreciated and long cherished by Bro. Miles' family. He wished him many years of further service. The remarks of the district chief were ably supported by Brethren Baskerville, D.P.C.S., James Parr di strict treasurer), J. Edwardson (worthy master), J. Adam, P.P.C.S. and the Lodge Secretary. The District Secre- tary Mr. Peter Edgar observed that he had known Bro. Miles for many years, and he had noticed with considerable interest and pleasure his constant and regular attendance at the lodge meetings, and his unfailing interest in all matters that affected the members of the Order. Through evil report and good report he had ever been a faithful supporter of every movement for the good of his fellow members. Bro. Miles on acknowledging the presentation, said he felt overwbelumed by all the kind things said about him. He was particularly struck by the hope expressed by all the speakers that he would continue in the future as in the past his attendance and service to the Lodge. He assured them that that this presentation would not make any difference to him in this respect. The reading of the minutes brought a- very successful meeting to a close.
