'LOCAL NEWS.

Serious Accident.

A. man named William Henry (20), of Whitworth, near Rochdale, was admitted into the Warrington Infirmary on Tuesday night, having broken his leg and sustained an injury to the ribs. It is understood that Whitworth was walking to Warrington, when a furniture van, coming down a hill, ran into him, and crushed him against a wall. He is progressing favourably. Clerical Change at Stockton Heath. The living of St. Thomas's, Stockton Heath (vacant by the appointment of the Rev. H. V. Pigot to the Rectory of Grappenhall) has been offered by the patron, Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., to the Rev. J. Collins, MA., of Annley, Leeds, by whom it has been accepted. The Rev. James Collins, MA. (Merton College, Oxford) was ordained deacon 1894, priest 1895 (Ripon), and was curate of St. Cuthbert, Hunslet, Leeds, from 1894 to 1807, and from 1897 to 1000 was at Christ Church, Walmsley, Bolton. Since the last ' named year he had been curate-in-chatge of the Mission Church, of St. Bartholomew Armley, Leeds, where he has done excellent work in many directions. ASK for CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLA.Sb MINERAL WATERS.—Advt. Vicar of Sankey and Sunday Newspapers. A few years ago, says " St. Mary's Great Saukey Magazine," we had occasion to enter a protest against the sale of Sunday newspapers in our village. The sanctity of the Sabbath we are well aware cannot be enfoiced on any individual, and we are perfectly well aware that we are living in a free country; and so on. At the same time we hold old-fashioned views as to the way in which Sunday should be Observed, and we .do not think that the moral tone of the village is in any way raised by the sale of Sunday papers, mostly sporting. Likely enough the " law allows it," but surely to everyone who has the moral as well as the temporal interest of our village at heart —and who is it that has not —to all such the sale of these papers ought to set him thinking. In saying this much we com- pletely exonerate our local agents. The sale is an importation. PUBLIC BENEFIT. UNDER_THE CLOCK FOR BEST

BOOTS IN LANCASHIRIL—Advt. The Headmastership of the ‘National Schools.

The managers of the Parish Church Boys' School, Church-street, have decided to appoint Mr. George Brown to the position of headmaster of the school, ' rendered vacant by the retirement of John Dewhurst Mr. Brown was selected out of 130 candidates, and there is no doubt that the apimintment is a wise one and one which will give general satisfaction. In his earlier years Mr. Brown was a scholar and then a pupil teacher at the National School. After passing through college ho obtained an appointment at Oldham which he later resigned in order to come to Warring- ton as an assistant master to Mr. Dewhurst. Some years ago he left Warrington to take up the head- mastership of St. Stephen's School, Liverpool, greatly raising the status of the school during his work there. The managers had another vacancy to fill , and appointed Mrs. Singleton, of St. Peter's Newton-le- Willows, head mistress of the Ellesmere-street Infant School to succeed Miss Dewhurst. Mrs. Singleton was a pupil at the Girls' School and subsequently a pupil teacher at the Infants' School,conducted by Miss Shaw. After a successful collegiate training she then became assistant to Mr. Dewhurst at the boys' school in Church-street, where she had charge of Standard I. and did excellent work.

KITCHEN RANGES, Fire Grates, Files &c., large stock.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge-street, Warrillgton.—Aarr.

Re-opening of Holy Trinity Church. After having been closed for some weeks for re- decoration Holy Trinity Church was re-opened on Thursday night when a special service was held. I There was a large congregation. The service was conducted by the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton. During I the evening the anthem "Send out Thy light " (Gounod, was ably sung by the choir. The preacher was the Rev. J. W. Dearnley who took for his text the 211th and 27th verses of the 10th Chapter of St. Luke. The Bible,', he said, was not to be regarded 89 a book but as the revelation of God. People could criticise the Bible, they could even take , away the Bible, but they could not take away the ' revelation of God which was given in the book. The revelation of God was inviolable and indestructible and they must get it from the Bible into their hearts. The services will be continued on Sunday when the preachers will be, in the morning the Rev. Wilfrid Stanton. and in the evening the Rev. T. T.Lee Jones,vicar of Dares- bury. Commenting on the renovation and re-lighting of the church, the Holy Trinity Parish Magazine says ; —" We think it will be admitted that the church has never looked better. Messrs. Hesketh and Son, Ltd., under the direction of our old friend, Mr. John Hesketh, have aimed at making the interior attractive, while retaining the churchlike appearance which, we feel sure, both clergy and congregation desire, •Paint has been used almost entirely instead of colouring (at, of course, considerably increased expense) as hitherto. 'ln addition, the brass-work has been xe-lacquered, and some addition has been made to the heating ser- vice pipes. With respect to the re-lighting, we hope and believe this will be entirely satisfactory. To defray the deficiency(aboutlBo)on the re-lighting fund, and what is required for the renovation, after taking into account the amount in band from the yearly sum set aside since the last renovation, the churchwardens confidently look forward to generous assistance from the congregation and friends of the church. to an -Elder.

Ur. W. E. Rees, who has been connected with St. John's, as an elder and Sunday school teacher, for several years, has just severed Ids connection with the Church, being about to leave Warrington for Dublin. On Wednesday evening last the minister and managers J met in the vestry for the purpose of presenting to Mr. Rees a number of theological volumes, as a tangible j expression of regard. The minister, the Rev. J. L. ' Milne, 8.D., occupied the chair, and after opening the proceedings, called on Mr. P. H. Brown to make the presentation. In doing so Mr. Brown referred to the high christian character of the departing elder, his valuable services to St. John's and his many good qualities as a church officer-bearer and personal friend. He assured Mr. Rees that they all much regretted his departure from amongst them, but they wished him success where he j was going, and they had the satisfaction of knowing j that what was their loss would be some other church's J gain. They knew he was a close and intelligent j student of the bible, and they hoped that the volumes they now asked him to accept as some—though very inadequate—expression of their regard, might be use- ful to him in his studies as well as help him to remember the many friends and well- wishers be was leaving behind in Warrington. All present heartily endorsed Mr. Brown's remarks. Mr. Rees replied very feelingly, warmly thanking the minister and his fellow office-bearers for the handsome and valuable volumes, and for their very kind and complimentary remarks concerning himself. He would never forget the many good friends he had made at St. John's, and he felt very much the parting from the minister and congregation. Mr. Rees has also been presented with a writing desk by the sorholars of the Sunday school, in which he had been a' teacher during the whole period of his connection with St. John's.

The Memorial to Fallen Soldiers. At the conclusion of the ordinary business at the Town Council meeting on Tuesday, Councillor Wright asked the Mayor to inform the subscribers to the Soldiers' Memorial Fund what progress had been made with the object of that fiond.—The Mayor said this was not a matter for the Town COuncil to discuss, but he would answer the question as a matter of courtesy. Some delay had been occasioned as the sculptor had only just been supplied with details of the soldiers' clothing, but the work would now be proceeded with as quickly as possible. That Was the position of affairs as he understood them from the Town Clerk. Alderman Bennett at Leige. • . The Warrington Chamber of Commerce is being represented at the autumnal meeting of Associated Chambers of Commerce, at Leige, Belguirn, by the secretary (Alderman Bennett). The chamber had given notice that it would move the Mowing resolu- tion :—" In view of the heavy disability imposed upon industries and commerce in the United Kingdom by the excessive cost of inland transport, included in which are the waterways of the county, this committee earnestly calls upon His Majesty's Government to take steps for the full utilization, co-ordination and expan- sion of British and Irish Canals, either by the establishment of a public canal trust or by some other method calculated to create a national system of cheap inland water transportation." The resolution was however withdrawn in favour of one moved by Sir John Brunner, M.P., which embodied its essential principles. It was decided to appoint an influential deputation to urge the importance of the matter upon the Board of Trade. PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST. BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Wedding at the Parish Church. An interesting wedding was solemnized at the Parish Church on Thursday, the parties being Miss Ellen Clarke, second daughtet of Mr. John Clarke, Manchester-road, and Mr. Aarcin Sitnkin, son of the late Mr. Simkin, Mersey Mount, Warrington. Both parties are well known, Mr. Simkin being a prominent member of the Parish Church choir. The ceremony was performed by the Rector in the presence of a large congregation. ' The bride, who was given away by her father, was attired in a dress of fawn voile and wore a biscuit-coloured hat. Her bouquet was of]white lilies and:chrysanthemums. The bridesmaids were the Misses Annie and Mabel Clarke, sisters of the bride, who wore, the first-named a dress of reseda green voile and a black picture hat, and the latter a dress of pale blue voile and a white hat. They carried bouquets of carnations. The recep- tion was held at the house of the bride's father, after which the happy couple left for Llandudno where the honeymoon is being spent. Warrington Juvenile Rechabites. I The annual picnic' of John Dimond Unions Pride, Hope of Latchford. Onward Padgate, and Wycliffe juVenile tents took place on Saturday last, September. 2nd, the place selected being Frodsham Hills. A special train left Bank Quay station at 1.45, arriving at Frodsham about 2.15 p.m. The weather in the morning was anything but promising, but on arrival at Frodsham it was very much better than anticipated, the rain keeping off during the whole of the time. The children thoroughly enjoyed themselves in playing rounders, cricket, donkey riding, &c. as well as rambling on the bills picking blackberries. At 415 over 400 sat down to an excellent tea provided by the superintendents, in the.spacious refreshment rooms on Mersey View, to which everyone did ample justice. After tea various walks were organised ; some went to the caves, and others over the hills by Dunsclale Hollow (or Happy Valley.) Time and darkness com- pelled them to return to the station where the train was in waiting, which reached Warrington about 830 p.m. Everything passed off without a bitch, or accident of any kind to mar the proceadings, and it was the unanimous opinion of all present that it was the best annual juvenile picnic that they had ever had

REVISING THE LISTS.

Mr. J. T. Foard, revising barrister, opened the annual court for the revision of the voters' lists at the Town Hall this (Friday) morning. The number of votes on the register is 11,815 as compared with 11,693, an increase of 122. This year there are 159 claims and Si objections, total 244, against 171 claims ' and 92 objections ; total 263. Mr. Joseph Howes, jun.' appeared on behalf of the Liberal party, and Mr. W. H. Holbrook and Mr. Herbert Hayes repre- sented the Conservatives. MR. LRROLD TOFT'S CLAM

Mr. Harold Taft claimed an occu-P;tion vote in yes- pect of the shop, 6, Sankey-street. He stated that the shop was in his mother's name, but that he was in partnership. Mr. Holbrook said the shop was rated at ;Ea per; annum, and the business was carried on by the mother and son. There was no vpte on the list in respect of the shop but Mrs. Toft had a vote for the dwelling house. The business was left to the mother and children. Mr. Howes contended that the claimant was not a partner as there was no deed of partnership. The business was Mrs.Toft's. It was ascertained that the accounts Were made out to Sarah Jane Toft. _ _

The barrister said he should require farther evid- ence before he could allow a vote.

The case was adjourned.

CORPORATION EMPLOYEES' WAGES.

COUNCILLOR sToTr PROPOSES A MINIMUM OF 245.

Motion Described as Outrageous and Rejected.

The wages of Corporation employees were ander dis- cussion at the meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday evening, but Councillor Stott's proposal that every able-bodied workman over twenty-one years of age should receive a minimum weekly wage of 245. was rejected. Councillor STOTT'S proposition read as follows :

1. No able-boded inanremployed under the Corpora- tion, and having reached twenty-one years of age, shall be paid less than at the rate of 245. per week.

2. The foregoing standing order shall become opera- tive on the first day in October next In submitting the motion Councillor Stott said he had long thought a large nnm:)er of the employees of the Corporation were not paid a living wage. From information he had obtained from the Borough Treasurer and the gas manager that oninion had been strengthened. • The question was one of very great im- portance and he had intended to raise it ever since he became a member of the Council. The MAY 011: 1710. v do yoa want the proposal to work? You don't expect scavengers to be paid 21s. ' Councillor STOTT : I expect every able-bodied man working for the Corporation to receive that wage. The NIAXOft : Haven't you made a mistake? Don't you moan Ns. a day? (Laughter). Councillor STOTT : 1 don't sea your point of view. The MAYOR : Seventeen shillings is the ordinary wage given to the scavengers. To propose to give them 21s. without asking the committee is only wasting time.

Councillor ASHCROFT : Are the scavengers all able- bodied men?

- Councillor STOTT : I will explain what I mema if you will have patience. Continuing, he said he had gone to some little trouble and had been able to get ex- pert evidence taken after a lengthy consideration. He thought he should be able to prove to the Cou,cil that it was not possible for a man and his wife with a small family to use in decency and comf•irt on less than 245. a week. Councillor Stott then quoted an estimate of the weekly expenditure in the house of a workingman with three children, showing that 245. was easily ab- sorbed by the necessities of life: -

Alderman Prearotatr : You haven't put anything down for beer. (Laughter). Councillor STarr : No, there is no allowance for beer, tobacco. newsplpers or any holiday—only for what I consider to be the necessities of life and taking the Workhouse standard of food. The Mavoit : They ought all to go into the Work- house if that is the case. (Laughter). Councillor STorr : I shall not finish speaking for a I considerable time if you repeatedly interrupt. Councillor STorr said that the Corporation at present employed 43 men at not mare than 18s. per week., 83 at not less than 18s. and not more than £l, 47 at not less than Li abd not more than 225., 137 at not less than 228 and not more than 245. Of those numbers 23 were old men and his resolution excluded them. There were thus 237 employs receiving less than 245. a week. He had obtained figures as to the minimum rate of wages in a large namber of towns in England and Scotland and in not a single instance was the minimum as low as he was asking for. It was not an extravagsnt request to make, as he contended the Corporation ought to be model employers and not drag at the heels of any private individual. They ought to see that the men were paid a living wage; whereas with soma of the wages paid at the present time it was perfectly impos- sible for a workingman to bring up a family in comfort and decency. A raising of thestandard of wages weirild be to the advantage of shopkeepers and landlords who suffered most in poor districts when the people were badly paid. Even if his proposal had the effect of I increasing the rates Id. or 2d. he would not object. The Maxon observed that if Councillor Stott had wanted to bring the matter forward properly he would have moved the appointment of a committee to investi- gate as to whether the men were paid too little or not.! . . .

Councillor WRionT seconded the resolution. He said he felt it his duty to do so in view of the attitude he had taken on every possible occasion in opposing increases of salaries to officials until the Council raised the wages of the low-paid employs. He could speak with experience as a man who was responsible for bringing up a family of six. He should not know how to begin to keen a family of that size with 245. a week. He did not think the majority of members of the Council, even if they were single men, would know how to live on 245. a week and keep up their positions. The lklavolt : We have no gas works labourers on the Council at present. (Laughter.) Continuing he asked if it was contended that the Council paid any employes less than the standard rate of wages. Councillor STorr: There is no standard rate. We want to fix one.

Councillor Wwrojur (to the Mayor): I hope you will allow me to proceeU without unnecessary interruptions. You are only delaying the Council. Cries of "chair."

60-u-n-eillor4-n-t-cHT: "Chair" yourself. Continuing Councillor Wright said he axed the minimum living wage at 21s. Bd. a week and he wondered how many members of the Conceit would like to live on that amount. _

The )IATOR : I would not. Councillor WRIGHT • Then I hope you will support this motion. Continuing he said that although the minimum living wage was 21s. 83. per week the Cor- poration as public employers were paying men who were working for the public good a wage which was not sufficient to keep them in food equal to that supplied to paupers and criminals. Low wages were the cause of poverty,—(hear, hear)—physical deterioration, the high infantile mortality, Slums and slum life, and the decrease in the birth rate. How could they expect parents living in a state of semistarvation, to bring robust and healthy children into the world ? Mr. Wright proceeded to quote a large number of towns which had adopted a minimum wage, the figure in each case being higher than that proposed by Councillor Stott. The men employed by the Warrington Corporation were just as human as those employed in other towns. He claimed that the motion was so just and so modest that it was as little as they could do to accept it. Councillor BURTON said that personally he had no feeling with regard to the motion. He had, however, consulted a few workingmen in the town upon the matter, who were all of opinion that the Corporation employ& should be paid well, but they -could scarcely agree to 245. as the lowest standard, when they them- selves received only 21s. (" Shame.") They felt that while they received only Ll per week, it was not right that they should contribute their fair share to the rates to pay a similar class of men who were receiving more than they were. Alderman SMETHURST said the proposition was one of the most outrageous ever brought before the Council. They had had some "rum 'tins." but that beat all. 'Councillor Stott's resolution would mean an increased expenditure of £2,080, and as a Id. rate produced £833, the rates would be increased 23d. At the present time there were respectable shopkeepers who had very great difficulty in paying their rates. A better plan than raising wages would be to reduce the rates and indnce more manufactures to open works in the town and thus find additional employment. If they kept increasing the rates they would drive people out of the town like they had done in West Ham. That town had been almost ruined through the very same class of people who were proposing such schemes as this. He asked the Council to consider the number of empty houses in some of the best streets of Warrington due to the fact that people had left the town to go where rates were lower. In the name of the shopkeepers and those who had to struggle to pay the rates, he asked the Council to reject the proposition. Councillor Wn.soNi was of opinion that some standard of wages should be fixed for the workingmen but although he should heartily support Councillor Stott's resolution, he thought it might have been put in a more favourable form. He instanced how the Sanitary Works Committee recently decided that every man employed by them shonld commence at 21s. per week and after he had been in the employ of the Corporation a certain time he should be advanced to 225. If the man con- tinued to perform his duties satisfactorily he could apply to the committee and if they thought he was worth a little more they would grant it. It must not be forgotten that men receiving S 1 or 18s. from a private firm were able to make a great deal of overtime, whereas the Corporation eruplo3ds received nothing but the bare wage. The Mayon said he did not think any member of the Council desired a Corporation employs not to be paid full value for his work. He had not hoard of a single committee, where it was complained that workmen were under-paid. Only the other day the Council raised the tram-drivers and conductors to a very high standard, considering the work done. It would have been better if Councillor Stott, instead of asking for a general standard, had moved the appointment of a small com- mittee to inquire into any department which bethought was not paying the proper rate. The wages of ordinary labourers were regulated by supply and demand. If a man was a good workman he received good wages but if he was a poor workman and " slung ' his time he f generally ended by working for the Corporation. —Councillor Stott's proposals would mean an increase in the rates of 2d. as near as could be reckoned. Councillor t VAS said he was in sympathy with a a fixed standard, but whether Councillor Stott's Was the best way of putting the matter WAS another question. The matter had come up again and again but it had always turned on the old question of the standard rate of wages, He was in somewhat of a dilemna as he did not represent organized labour alone but the whole of the ratepayers, whereas the resolution would benefit a certain number of Corporation employes and others who , did not work for the Corixwation.While he was anxious , that something should be done, there were undoubtedly difficulties in the way. The old men and those who were not able-bodied wouldlave to be dealt with.

Councillor BARTON said the resolution would to some extent interfere with the working of the committees. Only that evening he was told by some burgess of his ward that if the resolution were carried a number of men were ready to rush to Corporation employment to secure the als. He wished Councillor Stott had introduced a resolution that would have done more to meet the views of the Councillors who sympathised with under-paid workmen.

I Councillor Man INN said that if the Corporation were going to enter into competition with private employers of labour they would soon have a great crowd of Cor- poration labourers. If it could be proved that the Corporation were paying a single employ 6 less than a man in a similar position anywhere else in the town was receiving, he (Councillor Maginn) was prepared to raise his wages. If Councillor Stott's motion were carried, the poor labourer who worked at the forgo for £1 Os. 7d. I would have to contribute to pay Corporation servants , more than he himself received. Where was the honesty and straightforwardness of such a proposal ? Copora- tion workmen had not to work as hard as those in similar , positions in works. The Council as representatives of ,I all sections of the community had to do justice to all. If trade improved the Council would no doubt be pre- pared in a short time to raise the rate of wages. It I was madness to ask the Council to vote an im iediate !increase from 17s. to 245. per week in some cases. The man who received 17s. per week had his working clothes provided and he was not overworked. He also received the wages regularly Councillor Srorr then replied on the discussion, and pointed out that he had placed the subject before three public meetings, and there had not been a dissentient I voice. Workingmen who were receiving 17s. and 18s. I per week had entirely approved of his action. Alder- man Smethurst thought the resolution NV11.3 outrageous, but he (Councillor Stott) considered it very modest. He considered it an outrage that Alderman Smethurst should make such a statement. If he (Councillor Stott) felt sure the rates would be increased to the extent Alderman Smethurst suggested, he should consider it showed a greater need for the changed conditions. If it was said that he was moving the resolution as an advertisement or to pander to the electorate, he would remind Councillor Maginn that he was not in a position to give picnics to gain public popularity. The MAYOR: Everything comes here for popu- larity. Councillor MAGINN : lam very glad to have adver- tised myself in that way. I am very sorry I have not been able to be more popular and give another picnic. (Laughter.) I hope things will be better. Councillor Srorr concluded by saying that he was not going to take the standard of any private employer who was satisfied to pay 18s. per week as the standard for Corporatiou employees. THE WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY'S NEXT OPERA. _ The Mikado.

As will be seen from an announcement in our advertising columns the Warrington Musical Society is following up the big successes of the past two years with the production of probably the greatest favorite of the Gilbert-Sullivan series of operas— "The Mikado." This is to be performed on a most complete and magnificent scale in Parr Hall on six nights c-immencing Monday October 16th, and it is quite safe to prophesy that the event will arouse great public- interest. We understand that the rehearsals, already far advanced, have been resumed this week after the vacation holiday, and that the enthusiasm of all concerned is such as to promise a really fine presentation of the work. As in the past, the training of the opera company is in the joint hands of Mr. F. H. Crossley as Conductor and Musical Director, and of "ffr, Thornley-Dodge as Stage-Manager and Dramatic Instructor. Appended is a list of the Dramatis Personm.:— THE MIKADO. The Mikado Mr. Fred Fieldhouse. Nanki-Poo Mr. J. Rabbitt.

WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY. A Revelw of the Past Season. WARRINGTON'S MUsICAL TASTE.

The annual general meeting of the Warrington Musical Society will be held in the Cairo-street school- room on Monday evening next under the presidency of Mr. F. Monks, vice-president of the society. In his annual report which has just been issued to the mem- bers the hon. secretary (Mr. W. E. Atkin) gives an interesting review of last year's work. We make the following extracts :

===== 0002601_19050909_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
LENGTH OF SEASON.

The actual concert work occupied .ft slightly shorter period than was the case in the previous season, the first rehearsal being on September 20th, 1904, and the final concert on February 28th, 1005, or about one month earlier than the 1903-4 season which closed on March 23rd, 1901. When it is remembered that the Con- ductor and many of the members of the society are actively at work with opera rehearsals almost immedi- ately after the final concerts and that the opera per- formances take place in October, it is seen that for A section of the society at least the work is almost con- tinuous throughout the year.

===== 0002601_19050909_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
ATTENDANCE AT RETIE A. RS ALS.

The importance of regular attendan.cea-t the practices has so frequently been referred to in this annual report that it is unnecessary, I trust, to#emphasize previous remarks. On the whole the numbers at each practice throughout the season were satisfactory, and I believe the Registrar is able to state that an i nprovement is shown by his books as compared with other seasons in this respect.

CONCERTS.

WARRINGTON'S MUSICAL TASTE.

The usual three Subscription Concerts were as follow :

Tuesday, November 15th, 1904 : "The Svvans and Skylark " (Goring Thomas). Tuesday, December 20th, 1904: " The Messiah." Tuesday, February 23th, 1905 : A miscellaneous programme. The appetite of the public for music of a class worthy of such a society as the Warrington Musical Society is apparently generally feeble, and not only in this town but almost everywhere the taste for oratorio or the concerted music of good composers for platform per- formance seems to exist to such a small degree as to render concert success financially a thing impossible. The difficulty of catering is almost insuperable, and your committee can only hope for a recovery of the healthy appetite to enable it to serve up its dishes with confidence of their being appreciated. At present the selection of any patticiilar work is attended with an enormous amount of speculation as to its reception by an audience. The oratorios " Elijah " and " The Messiah" are no longer certain of attracting an audi- ence anywhere, and many excellent works by modern composers cannot be thought of because of the feared financial result. The only feature of change in the used course of events was the introduction of a specially miscellaneous and chiefly orchestral pro- gramme, including several concerted vocal items by members of the society at the final concert on February 21st last. We believe this programme to have been heartily approved by those attending the concert.

THE OPERA PERFORMANCES. The opera section of the society was again most remarkably and deservedly successful in the performances of " The Gondoliers" in October last, and the com- mittee is once more conscious of its debt of gratitude to the performers and the Conductor, Opera Committee and all concerned for the invaluable help given to the funds of the society by them. The successful result of the two operas has enabled the Treasurer to invest a sum of money which is no mean start for a reserve fund, an object which the committee has had in view for long, but till now never been able to attempt.

===== 0002601_19050909_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
NUMBER OF MEMBERS.

The books show for 1904-5 :—The chorus : Sopranos 66, or 10 more than previous season; contmltos43,same as previous season ; tenors 23, 3 more than previous season; basses 37. 3 more than previous season ' • total 169, or 16 more than previous season : The orchestra : 33, same as previous season ; total iO2 members. THE WORK OF Tim CORMITTFE _

has been carried out in the same disinterested and hearty mfinner as always, and the attendance of mem- bers at its meetings has been all that could be desired. It is not called upon often to deliberate but its work is no sinecure, and the matters for decision are often of weight enough to make such meetings long and trying, but it is satisfactory in a very high degree to be able to report a thorough unanimity. It is my pleasure to offer to the committee, the conductor, and all the official and non-official members of the society, mY most sincere thanks for the constant and kindly help have always met in the carrying out of my pleasant duties.

===== 0002601_19050909_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====

THE CHARGES AGAINST COUNCILLORS. APPLICATION TO TAKE LEGAL PROCEEDINGS.

" To Clear the Honour of the Council."

By a unanimous vote the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday evening confirmed the decision arrived at in General Purposes Committee to apply to the Attorney General for leave to institute legal proceedings against two councillors for alleged corrupt practices. The Mayor formally movel the confirmation of the General Purposes Committee's recommendation which read as follows :—" The report of the sub-committee appointed to investigate and report on the charges of corrupt practices against two members of the Council was read ; and it was resolved that on the recommen- dation of the sub-committee the Town Clerk be directed to apply to the Attorney-General for leave to institute proceedings against the two Councillors involved under the Public Bodies Corrupt Practices Act, 1889." Alderman SMETHURST seconded.

Alderman PIERPOINT, Chairman of the Investigation Comraittea, said he thought the resolution of the com- mittee was all right so far as it went, but he would like to make an addition to it. He thought the Investiga- tion Committee should at least have a voice in the statement of the Case which was to be sent to the Attorney-General in applying for leave to prosecute. He did not think the Town Clerk would object to the committee being called in in consultation as to the way the case was put and he begged to move the addi- tion of these words to the resolution : subject to the statement of the case being submitted to the In- vestigation Committee for their approval and sugges- tions." He hoped the Council would agree to that addition and he saw no reftoon why they should not. The 14avoR: I understand from the Town Clerk that this matter cannot be discussed.

Alderman PIERPOINT : lam not discussing it. I am. only suggesting the addition of a few words to the resolution arrived at by the General Purposes Com- mitteee. Unfortunately or fortunately I was away taking a holiday when it was passed at the committee meeting, but if I had been there I should have suggested the addition of such words as I have mentioned just now, and I think the committee and the Town Clerk I would not have objected to them. The MAYOR.: But the addition does not interfere with the resolution. It will be the same if we pass this resolution as it is, and then tell the Town Clerk to do what you want. So far as submitting the case is ' concerned, it will not alter matters. It would simply , have to be laid before the Attorney-General whether you see it or not. 1 Alderman PIERPOINT : I don't wish to raise any discussion, because I don't think one would be at all useful. But Ido think that the Deputy Town Clerk, who was the legal adviser of the committee during the whole of the proceedings—and he acted in a manner entirely to the satisfaction of the committee, and in such a manner also as reflected credit upon his profes- sional judgment and acumen—should go on with the management of this. Alderman EVANS here made some remark to the speaker. Alderman PiErteom (continuing) : I do not want to raise a discussion, but if Alderman Evans would like me to suggest what I mean or explain myself more plainly,l will do so. lam not afraid of the consequences if other people are. Alderman SmErlicasr : We are not going to have a discussion. _

The Mtsoi: I cannot help myself. He will have his own way. Alderman PIERPOINT : I shall not do anything con- trail, to what you wish and I don't want to do anything ' contrary to the wish of the Council, but what I am , anxious about is this. The honour of the Conncil has been asssiled, at all events, and I want everything done that will carry the clearing of it to its fullest extent. The Town Clerk will, in all probability, be called as a witness in one of these cases, and therefore r think he himself will admit, at once that perhaps as he is going to be a witness he is perhaps not the fittest to draw up a statement of the case. I certainly am of opinion that th 4 is so. At, this point the TOWN CLERK, who was out of the r)om when the discussion commenced, returned to his seat, and the Mayor explained the proposal that the committee should see his statement of the case.

The N1...v0n: The Town Clerk says he does not object to it at all. Alderman PIERPOINT : Exactly. I was sure he would see exactly what I meant. What I rnggest. Ido in the best interests of the Corporation of Warrington, and have no desire to act prejudicially to anybody's interest. T only want to clear the fair fame of the Council. which has been assailed for many years past. The Towx CLIIRK : I understand ;chat you want is that when I have prepared the application to the Attorney-General the committee shall see it.

Alderman PIERPOIN'T Yes; that is it. Alderman PIERPOINT I suggest the Investigation Committee should go through the statement of the case with you and if they can make any suggestions, subject to agreement between ourselves and yourself, the whole thing can be amicably dory:. The Tows CLERK: r have not the slightest objec- tion to that, but I should object to any interference from the committee with my conduct of the case.

Aldernaon PIRRPOINT : Then it comes to this : that if the statement of the case as laid down by the Town Clerk does not fall in entirely with the views of the Investigation Committee we ourselves can take such action as we think proper. The MAYOR : This Council appointed a snb-corr- mittee to investigate and report. They did that very elaborately and did their best to get at the bottom of the affair. There were a great many amateur lawyers and cross-examinations of a peculiar character. They made a report out and it was decided to submit it to a barrister. He reported that under certain conditions it ought to be laid before the Attorney-General.The whole. committee signed that document and fell in with it. WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The . Vagrancy _Problem.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday morning, Mr. J. Billings presiding. There were also present :—Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broadbent, Rev. Dr. Mercer, Dr:Murray, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, G. Mather, G. Thomp- son. R. Flanagan, S. Davies, Mr. W. Seton, .1. C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awhery (master), A. Wright (superinten- dent of Padgate schools)..

THE VAGRANCY PROBLEM.

The CLERK reported that at a conference of Guardians held at Lancaster, on September Ist. under th. presi- dency of the Right Hon. Sir J. T. Hibbert, a resolution was passed to the effect that the Conference was of opinion that the provision of farm and labour colonies under State control, or under a coinbination of Boards of Guardians of a county or of a combination of coun- ties for the more or less lengthy detention of habitual tramps offered the best prospect of a favourable solu- tion of the question, and urging the necessary legislation for the purpose. The conference urged upon the public the undesirability of bestowing indiscrimate alms as it tended to foster the evil of vagrancy which it was desired to lessen. The attention of Boards of Guar- dians was drawn to the necessity of providing casuals with a mid-day meal before leaving the wards, ancl Boards within the North Western Poor Law Conference dictrict. &c., were requested to appoint a representative to serve upon an inter-county committee for the pur- pose of devising the best means of carrying into effect the resolutions passed at the Conference. The CLERK remarked that the conference was a most useful and practical one. Mr. THOMPSON suggested that it would be advisable ' to discuss the matter at the next meeting when there would he a large attendance. Mr. MATHEu hardly thought that step necessary as the matter was not a very important one so far as pounds, shillings and pence were concerned. The CLERK said it was the understanding that import- ant business of that kind should only be taken after duo notice had been given. It was therefore agreed to discuss the question at. the next meeting.

THE WATERWORKS CONTRACT.

ALL DOCUMENTS TO BE LAID BEFORE THE COUNCIL.

Policy of Secrecy Condemned.

SERIOUS ACCUSATION AGAINST COUNCILLOR PEMBERTON.

The longest and most heated discuision at the meet- ing of the Town Council on Tuesday evening centred on the vexed question of the waterworks contract. Excep- tion was taken by several Councillors to the choice of an arbitrator to settle disputed items in the contractor's claim being left in the hands of the Town Clerk as recommended by the Water Committee, and an amendment that the matter be referred back was carried by a large majority. At a later stage the Mayor undertook to call a meet- ing of the General Purposes Committee on Friday and place the whole of the documents relating to the contract before them. On that understanding Coun- cillor Pemberton withdrew a motion that an Investiga- tion Committee be appointed to g into the matter of the contract, as he was assured that if it was con- sidered necessary the committee could be appointed

at Friday's meeting. The minutes which the Council were naked to confirm

read as follows: At a meeting of the Water Com- ' mitteeheld on July 31st, the Town Clerk reported that the contract and specifications belonging to Mr. Lawson's contract had been produced to the Investiga- tion Committee as requested. The Town Clerk also reported that hehad had an interview with Mr. James Lawson with reference to his claim against the Cor- poration in connection with the Delph-lane contract, and that while having settled many of the items, it would be necessary to proceed to arbitration on some, and that he had agreed with Mr. Lawson that the refer- ence should be a friendly one so as to minimize thei cost as much as possible, It was resolved: "That the action of the Town Clerk he approved, and the choice of an arbitrator left in his hands." At a meeting of the same committee on August 21st., the Water Engineer laid before the meeting the con- tract, plans, bills, accounts, correspondence, and papers, in connection with Mr. Lawson's contract, and the committee requested Councillor Pemberton to give a list of the details which he required, and which would be furnished to him by the engineer. Mr. Councillor Pemberton declined to give this information, and after further discussion it was resolved: "That the docu- ments and papers be deposited in the Deputy Town Clerk's office and that Mr: Councillor Pemberton be allowed to examine them and to make extracts there- from providing the same are shown to the Mayor and Chairman." It was also resolved "That Mr. Coun- cillor Pemberton's solicitor be allowed to accompany hi m." On the 28th of August the committee resolved "That in Mr. Councillor Pemberton's examination of the papers in connection with Lawson's contract he be allowed to be accompanied by any member of the Council provided that such member (if not a member of the Water Committee) does not have access to any of the papers concerned." Alderman Fad:WOMB formally moved the adoption of the minutes. Alderman Evaxs seconded. Councillor BENSON said he would move an amend- ment " That the action of the Town Clerk be not approved, and that a committee be appointed— The MAYOR (interposing) : You can't do that. You can only move that the whole lot be referted back. Alderman PIERPOINT suggested that the Chairman of the Water Committee should explain his reason for bringing forward those minutes. It was rather unfair to the Council to keep the matter in its present position. In accordance with the Mayor's ruling, Councillor BENSON moved, as an amendment, "That the minutes be referred back." He took it that the Council were asked to pass them without knowing what they were sanctioning. Councillor LORD seconded the amendment, and thought that the Council should have some more information on the matter. The Town Clerk might Nave settled £20,000 or £30,000 for anything they knee, 'and the items outstanding might be a matter of a .£3 Dote. The information was most scanty. Councillor PEMBERTON asked, in view of the Mayor's rifling, if he contended that the Council had no power to appoint an arbitrator that night? Ile MAYOR said that the Water Committee did its own business, and stood by its own resolutiens. The Council could discuss their minutes and send them back if they. thoueht fit. But the committee conducted its own business in its own way. Councillor PEMBERTON : Can T move that this Council appoint a committee to appoint an arbitrator? The MAYOR: No; you don't know what you are going to arbitrate about. You can ask the Water Committee. Councillor PEMBERTON : Surely the Town Council is above the Water Committee. The MAYOR: Yes. If you don't like them you can sack them and get a fresh lot. Councillor PEMBERTON said he would support the amendment. Alderman TINNION submitted, with all due respect to the Mayor and the Town Clerk, that it was possible to move an amendment in the form that, Councillor Benson had suggested. If not, he thought that they should have a definite ruling as to why. There appeared from the ruling to be only two ways of opposing the resolu- tions—by referring them back or not confirming them. The MAYOR: The Water Committee have arrived at a deoision and sent that to the Council. If you don't like the decision you can send the minutes back, and the committee can re-consider it, but you can't alter it. Alderman FINNION sajd, with all due respect to the Mayor and the Town Clerk, he knew cases which went to Drove differently. The MAYOR: Then they have been irregular. That's my ruling, and that settles it ! Alderman TINNION : I hope it will not be found wrong on examination. The MAYOR: I will stand by what I say. Councillor PEMBERTON said the resolution of the Water Committee was more far-reaching than perhaps runny members would think. For all they knew, the items settled by the Town Clerk might amount to £20,000. If the Town Council passed the resol tions before them they would approve of all the items the Town Clerk settled with Lawson, and for ever after- wards must hold their peace. That was a very serious matter indeed, far more -serious than a great many members were aware off The contract with Lawson was the largest ever entered into by that Council, end the sum of money was the largest they had been called upon to pay for any contract. Ile thought that the members of the Council and the public of Warrington would he staggered when they got to know the amount of Lawson's claim. To spite of what had been said by the Chairman and the Town Clerk, practical men would substantiate the statement that the contract had been let for £24,400. Alderman FRANCOMB Tell the whole truth ! Councillor PEMBERTON : I am telling the whole truth. Alderman FRANCOMB You are only telling half the truth. Councillor PEMBERTON : said he had brought with him Alderman Franccmb's own statements in the Town Con '1 which he had taken the trouble to get for two yearFback. It was disgusting to come there and re- peatedly be given the lie by Alderman Francomb. It was grossly unfair and he challenged the Alderman to prove that what he (Councillor Pemberton) said was untrue. He asked for the ordinary fairplay of an Eng- lishman. The Mavoß : You probably misunderstand what he means. The contract was for £24,400 under certain conditions. Councillor PEMBERTON : The same conditions that apply to every contract. The MANOR The contract was for £21,400 under certain conditions. Councillor PE:WI-MAXON : That is quite right. So is every contract. The conditions, schedule of prices, and everything are the same as in every contract. It is monstrous that this misrepresentation should have gone on and that I should be made out to be a perfect liar. It is perfectly intolerable. The MAYOR : I should not like to say anything was perfect about this job.

The Examiner, Saturday September 9, 1905. WEAK, THIN AND PALE.'

Councillor PEMBRRTON : I think you would be well advised to say nothing about it—at all events to say less than you have said before. If the matter is investi- gated as it ought to be, it will be proved who tells the lies. I ask this Council to seriously consider the amendment. Continuing, he said that when he stated that the total bill was £50,000 it was said by the chair- man of the committee that he was telling untruths and the Town Clerk backed the chairman up. What he (Councillor Pemberton) said was nat. true,as the amount of the bill was actually higher than he stated. He did not think the Council would regret doing what he asked.

Alderman FRANCOMB said he was exceedingly sorry that these things were continually brought up in the way of trying to contradict each other. If there was OOP thing he admired in this world it was the man who told the truth. He did not mean to say that Councillor Pemberton was telling untruths, but he only told part of the truth. It had been explained month after month that the contract was an extraordinary one. Councillor PEM BERTON : Hear, hear. . .

_ Alierman FRANCOMB: Don't listen to his humbug. He is only catering to the gods or something like that. Continuing the alderman said the wells constructed by Lawson were more like coal 9its than anything else,and gave the dimensions. That enormous work was under- taken for £24,400 but that was only one part of the con- tract. If a certain quantity of water were found the Council had to pay so much per 1,000 gallons and if they I pumped an extraordinary quantity of water they had to 'pay for the hire of plant so as to keep the contractor from being absolutely ruined and his men from being drowned. If they had met with only the quantity of water estimated by Professor Boyd Dawkin's—one and a quarter million gallons per day—they would have had nothing but the £24,400 to pay. They, however, got more than double that quantity. They had pumped over 2 million gallons per day—in fact he believed the wells had been pumped to the extent of 4 million gallons per day. Because they were success- ful they had this bull dog treatment from Councillor Pemberton. It was really disgusting to be blackguarded because they had done their best and because they had a successful undertaking. If they talked to any manu- facturer in the town he would tell them that if the work cost £35,000 or even £50,000 or £OO,OOO the town ought to be thankful to get such a wonderful water supply. He did not mind the minutes being sent back if it would he more convenient to Councillor Pember- ton to discuss the matter next month. It would certainly be nearer November, and it might help him over the style. He (Alderman Prancomb) had never refused to answer any questions Councillor Pemberton had brought there. .

Councillor PEMBERTON : You have. Alderman FRANCOMB : Never in my life. Councillor PEMBFIRTON : You refused to give me the price of Lawson's bill. Alderman FRANCOMB I hadn't it myself. How can I possibly give Councillor Pemberton or anyone else the price of Laa-son's bill when I have not got it myself? It is in dispute at this moment and if we are wise people I think we ought to leave it in the hands of oar Engineer and the Town Clerk. They are surely more able to deal with it than anyone else. lam assured there has been a clerk of the works down the wells to see everything done. They have cot every item down and if you will look at the scherlale of prices you will see that if the water is to be lifted 70 feet there is one price ; 80 feet another price ; and 100 feet another. It is a most difficult and complicated piece of business and I do not doubt that Warrington will be safeguarded by the Town Clerk and Water Engineer. duncillor IVAGINN said he did not take sides either with Councillor Pemberton or Alderman Fraucomb. He was not making any charges against the Water Committee or the chairman of the committee, and was not going to say whether they had paid too much or too little. The matter had been before the council month after month but for some reason or other they could not get anything like lucid information. They conld not be told the amount which was in dispute. When asked for information the chairman of the com- mittee said he did not know the amount in dispute. They had just condemned the Fire Engine Committee for becoming responsible for a bill before coming to the Council for sanction and the same thing was being done by the Water Committee. He ventured to think not half the members of the Water Committee,who were supposed to be handling that affair knew the amount in dispute. They did not know and if they as a Water Committee would not take the responsibility how could they expect the council to sit there and allow them to akhnfile out of their responsibility for the account? They came to the Council and said " Gentlemen we know nothing about it ourselves ; will you pass it ? " That was not the way to conduct the town's affairs. If a private individual undertook a job of the size which the Water Committee had taken on he would scarcely risk leaving the settling of the account to his manac.er and the contractor. (Hear, hear.) If the Water Committee were not to give the Council details they should take the responsibility individually and collectively and say "We know the amount in dispute, and are prepared to settle." Not a Isingle member of the committee knew the amount in dispute,and therefore he was:bound to support the a mend- ment.

Councillor STorr said that not a single argument had been given why the members of the committee should not know the matters in dispute. The way matters had been going on for the past few months had created a strong suspicion in the mind of the public that every- thing had not been straightforward, and they as a Coun- cil should do all they could to remove that feeling. He thought the Council should know much more about the contract than they had been permitted to know. He was not speaking in favour of Councillor Pemberton, and did not agree with the way he went about business, but he must say that so far he hardly thought that gentleman had had fair play. The MaYea. : I cannot allow that. He gets it here if lam present. He gets it on committees of which I am a member. Councillor Pemberton cannot deny that he has had all the papers. . .

Councillor PEMBERTON : But Ido deny it. Councillor STorr : He asked for certain documents. The MAYOR : He has had them. Councillor STOTT : The Chairman himself has stated that he has not seen the documents.

The MAYOR : They were not in a position to be laid before the committee. They were not complete. Councillor STOTT : Councillor Pemberton has only had the vportunity of seeing the documents since the last Council meeting but he has asked for them for a long time, whereas there has not been a single reason given why they should not have been produced earlier. It his been urged that the contractor declined to allow the documents to be produced, but I have still to learn what power Nit-. Lawson has to prevent certain docu- ments belonging to the Corporation being seen by members of the Corporation. Continuing, he said he felt strongly on the matter. He was dissatisfied and disgusted with the action and the inaction of the Water Committee.

The MAYOR: If you like I will call a special Council meeting. There is a cart load of documents, and if you will go through them you will he welcome. Councillor Slam': I don't want to go through them, but those members who wish to see them ought to have an opportunity for doing so. If they cannot have them they are not fit to be trusted by the ratepayers. Alderman PIERPOINT observed that certain documents required for the purposes of the Investigation Com- mittee were obtained because there was no power to keep them back. but they had been refused to Councillor Pemberton. This palicv of secretiveness was going to do the town no good. It had done it no good in the past and it would do it no good in the future. Let them be honest and straightforward, give the documents asked for, and if that was done all their difficulties would be at an end. It was only this policy of keeping something back that made people think that something was behind that ought not to be kept back. He had said before in that Council, and he would say it again. that Alderman Francomb ought to have given some information about the matter, and with reference to the necessity of the minute in question. As an ordinary member of the Council he (Alderman Pierpoint) did not see for the life of him why the committee could not have taken now' themselves tbe responsibility of appointing their own arbitrator. Did they think, as a man of business, that he would leave an important business matter to be settled by his manager? Certainly not. He hoped he had sufficient good sense to settle matters himself, and if the committee had been more open—he knew they knew nothing about the business because every member of the committee he had asked had told him that he knew nothing about the business—this trouble would not have arisen. It seemed to have been a mysterious packet no one had a right to open, and he thought it would be better to get rid of the policy of hiding things. Table them. They had had enough of this secretiveness. Let the committee give the Council an explanation, and if it was a reasonable one the Town Council were reasonable men and would not act in an unreasonable manner.

....... Councillor EvA.Ns said that the longer they kept these matters in abeyance the greater the suspicion in

he town became. He quite agreed with Alderman Pierpoint that the sooner theematter was ended, and the public made thoroughly acquainted with all the facts of the case, the better it would be for the town, and all concerned. The MA.YOR said that so far as he was concerned he had tried to put everything that was wanted before the Council. The bill was not in a proper state to come before the committee, if they asked for ten sorts of nails from an ironmonger and he sent an invoice for 20 no one present would pay straightaway. They would, not pay until it had been checked and it was the chock- ing oft that was being done. That was where all the trouble was. Until the matter had been checked all the mem- bers of the committee knew nothing about it. They had their arbitrator or engineer, who said there was only so much money owing. The contractor said he was entitled to a certain amount of work, and put on double what the engineer said, and thre the matter lay, and when the contractor started talking about fore- ' ing and said he must have his money it became a point of law. When a man threatened to put one in court action had to be taken. If any member of the Council wished to see the documents he would call a meeting of the Council, and they would have them all on the table.

Councillor MA GINN was pleased to hear the Mayor say that. Would he take the action he had named and satisfy everyona ? He presumed discussion would be allowed.

The NlAvoa : We can call a special meeting of the Council. I don't think the Water Committee would object to any of their business being brought before the Council if the Council wish it. _ _ _

Councillor Mao INN (lid not suggest that they should settle the matter before the public. A fill meeting of the .Council as a General Purposes Committee should be called to discuss the matter before it went to arbitra- tion.

The Afasoft said that all he wanted was that the Council should have a chance of inspecting the papers Councillor H VANS said that if a meeting of the whole of the Council was convened to investigate the matter it would go a long way towards clearing the air. (Hear hear.) Alderman TisNroN : It will simply be a waste of time and will be a mistake.

- The MAYOR : I-h;-ve--wasted a good deal of time already. A little more will do no harm. Alderman Tixstox; You have paid the Council the , compliment of suggesting that they could as a body come together and understand the papers. Excuse me if I differ from you. The members are not all water engineers and town clerks. In fact none of them are fit for either job, but they think they are. (Laughter.) The MAYOR: Would there be auy harm in seeing the papers ? Councillor M.s.ort•zr : Not a bit.

Alderman TINNLON, continuing, said he thought that all this secrecy was a mistake, but someone had been responsible for it. The dispute under any circumstances would have to be settled by arbitration, but the County Court judge had the power of nominating an arbitrator. I Instead of doing so it had been suggested that Lawson and the Town Clerk should agree upon an arbitrator. It was called friendly, but it would be none the less thorough, and would reduce to a minimum so far as they conld costly proceedings. No harm could be done by referring the matter back, and when it was introduced again lot them have all the circumstances and facts before them and then no one could carp about not having sufficiint information, and then they would have less wrangling and a little more common sense. (Hear, hear.) As far as vny member of the Water Committee could grasp the facts and impart there to the committee let him do so. In his opinion in the past the committee had made a great blunder. It had been a wrangle between the Chairman of the Committee and Councillor Pemberton. Who was responsible he did not know. It wa,t quite time the Council had finished with this sort of thing. If they had a little less wrangling, and intro- duced a little more commonsense into their debates, it would be more to the advantage and to the character of the town. It was time an end was put to this bitter wrangling, between one or two sections, end that the matter was treated in a 'proper business manner. He suggested that the matter be withdrawn, and referred back to the committee, and introduced again in such a form that no one could carp at. it. He did not suggest any reflection on the Town Clerk, and if be were the only person concerned, he should be pleased to allow the matter to rest in his hands—in the hands of the men whose brains they bought and paid for—without any reflection on the officials of the Water Committee. I The Tow.N CLERK said the Water Committee had been acting throughout under his advice as the legal adviser of the Corporation and he was, therefore, entirely responsible for the delay in the production of the documents. In his opinion the time was not ripe for the production of the documents until he bad gone through them with Mr. Lawson and Mr. Deas. He had gone through the papers and Mr. Dees had gone through the measurements made by Mr. Lawson and the Clerk of the Works had gone through them, and he had ascertained that the measurements were correct and those items could not be disputed. There were three main items which hadbeen referred to arbitration. They had already executed a contract and agreed to refer any items in dispute to that arbitrator. The modus operandi was this, Mr. Lawson's solicitor would sub- mit names of half-a-dozen arbitrators for him to select one from, and he should submit the list to the com- mittee, and take their advice as to who they thought the best, and Mr. Deas would say who he thought was the best to select. Beyond that the could not move. They had agreed to go to arbitration. They were bound to go, and it was not in the public interests to discuss these items here. Alderman PtEneora : Am Ito understand that the suggestion of an arbitrator has to come from Lawson? rhe TOWN CLERK: Or from me.

Iiie;1;;;PrERI"OINT : Then according to your state- ment Lawson has the power to appoint an arbitrator friendly to himself? Alderman TINNION : No. Alderman PIERPOINT : According to the Town Clerk's statement that is so. The TOWN CLERK: I am not bound to accept any gentleman nominated by Lawson. If Ido not approve of the name be submits I submit six to him. Councillor !..I.aoislN: But this is not coming to the point. The lavoß : I will see yon have the documents. That is coming to the point. (Laughter, and cries of " Vote, vote.") Councillor MAGINN: We don't want this thrashed out publicly, but it seems • that the members of the Town Council were not trusted. We must place all confidence in the Town Clerk, and in the Water Engineer, and we are r.ot trusted ourselves._ . . .

Councillor WILKINSON: Several members have spoken of their managers and cashiers. Imagine a tanner speak- ing on a point of law, or a builder! The Town Clerk is your legal adviser. The amendment of Councillor Benson was then put to the meeting, and carried by a large majority.

COUNCILLOR PEMBERTON AND THE D.OOUNIENTS. SUGGESTED INVESTIGATION, SERIOUS CHARGE AGAINST COUNCILLOR

PEMBERTON.

Councillor Pemberton had also given notice to move : "That a committee be appointed by this council. fortn- with to investigate and report to the Council upon all accounts and transactions between the Corporation and Mr. Lawson, in connection with his contracts at Delph- lane. When the motion was reached the MAYOR said to Councillor Pemberton : You know what I have decided about putting the papers out for inspection, will that satisfy you. Councillor PEEMBERTON : I want this commiitee appointing. You know it is practically impossible for the Council to investigate the papers as they lie on the table. The contract covers a period of three years. I am quite willing to move the adjournment of the Council for a week and have my motion discussed then. The Mayon : I will not adjourn the Council meeting, I will go on with it to-night. One night of this sort of business is enough in a month. This last hour bas been painful. I have agreed to fix Friday night for the examination of the paper and every member of the Council shall see them if he likes. Councillor NlAoisiN remarked that the meeting would be one of the General Purposes Committee, and when they had inspnted the documents they could decide whether to appoint the committee. Councillor EVANS said that if the Council felt it necessary to have a committee of inspection,Councillor Pemberton would not have an opportunity of moving his resolution. Councillor PEMBERTON : I have had permission from the Water Committee to inhpect the documents but I refuse to do it now. I would have done this, only a fortnight ago I found I am not safe in a committee of five. I have had a very serious accusation brought against me, as serious a case as any public man in ,England could have brought against him—a criminal

accusation. I decline to ever place myself in that position again. I appeal to the Town Council for the fairness and honesty we claim as Englishmen. I claim the protection of the Council. I say I ought to have that committee appointed and I ought to be able to investigate those accounts. It is a very serious matter —doubly serious now, for it affects my honour. Before it only affected the honour and the pockets of the rate- payers. Now it affects more than money can buy for me. That is why I want to insist that the committee should be appointed. As for the General Purposes Committee looking at the papers of a contract spread over three years, it is simply impossible. The very same thing was attempted a fortnight ago. At a special meeting of the Water Committee the papers were laid before the members. I commenced to purnse the contract. It took me about twenty minutes and I had only got half way through. Then other members began to be uneasy. It would have taken at least three or four days to go through the papers. The MAYOR (producing a document' : Here is the contract if you want to go through that. The others are matters of detail.

Councillor PEMBERTON : I appeal to you as English- men to give me fair play. The MAYOR : Well, I will give you Friday night. Go on ; I have done all I can for you. I will give it upnow.... -

'Councillor EVANS It does not alter the position. Councillor Pemberton will at tho adjourned meeting have the opportunity of moving the resolution. The members of the Council who have not up to the present had the opportunity of seeing the documents will have seen some of them and they way be strongly impressed with the necessity for the resolution. There was a considerable discussion as to the advis- ability of adjoining the Council meeting or calling a meeting of the General Purposes Committee as sug- gested by the Mayor. Eventuillv it was agreed that the Council should meet as a General Purposes Com- mittee on Friday when the papers will be produced and Councillor Pemberton will move the resolution if con- sidered necessary.

THE ITEALTH OF THE DISTRICT.

The Medical Officer reported that during the'month there had been 14 births and 31 deaths. The death rate was 335 per 1,000 as against 31.0 the average of the previous three months and 442 for the corresponding period of last year. Fifteen deaths occurred at the County Asylum exclusive of which the death rate proper was equal to 17.3 per 1000. Seven cases of scarlet fever had been reported.

THE SURVEYOR AND MOTOR CARS.

Mr. THOMPSON called the attention of the Council to the remarks which were made by the surveyor at the last meeting of the Council with regard to the damage to roads done by motor cars. Mr. Newcombe said he was most strongly of opinion that motor cars did not do anything like the amount of damage which was attributed to them. District Councils all over the country held an opinion which was exactly the reverse as were also the views which he held with regard to the damage and nuisance occasioned by motor cars. Mr. Thompson read extracts expressive of the opinions of surveyors with a number of Councils, all of which supported the opinion that motor cars did a large amount of damage. One gentleman said the surface of the road would not, now, through motor cars, last two- thirds the time they did previously. Mr. KNowLxs They are all of one opinion except Mr. Newcombe.

Mr. Newcombe' s espressiou of opinion was rather unwise. .

Mr. NEwcomum : It was asked and I gave it. The same opinion is held by the Surveyors to the Lancashire and Cheshire County Council. Mr. KxowLas thought it was unquestionably a fact that motor-cars did extensive damage. Mr. NEwcomrix : They undoubtedly do a certain amount of damage but not so much as is attributed to them. Mr. KNOWLES said he acknowledged that the Sur- veyor was entitled to his opinion, but knowing the feeling of the Council on the subject he thought it would have been more discreet on the part of Mi. Newcombe if he had referred to the Council before he published his opinion. Mr. NEWCOMBE said he never said motor cars did no damage. Motor cars driven without reason did damage but carefully driven cars did practically no damage. Mr. lisowLEs : And half the motor-cars on the roads are driven unreasonably.

A Veteran Tramper. Mr. Mark All, a veteran London engineer, who has undertaken to walk 60,000 miles with the object of demonstrating to employers that a man is not done with at the age of 45—for Mr. All is 78 —passed through Warrington on Wednesday morning. He had come on from Northwich and was pnceeding to Liverpool, thence to Ireland and finally to Russia. The veteran looks hale and hearty and still possesses a. springy step which bids fair to take him over his remaining balance of 15,064 miles. He started from Fleet street London on Auest 6th, 1900 and has since tramped through the British Isles five times, and through France, Italy, Germany, Switzerland, Belgium and Spain covering no less than 44,936. The grand tramp is to finish in Fleet street in December, 1907, when the plucky old man is guaranteed a sum of 500 by various journals and members of his society. Mr. All carries his engineering tools with him and takes on a job wherever he can get it, for he is forbidden to be and makes no attempt to do so. We are con—in ced that he will earn his precarious and humble living for many years Yet.

Stomach Troubles Reduced a Young Woman to This —Mother Seigel's Syrup Cured Her.

"Some years ago I derived great benefit from taking Mother Seigel's Syrup—in fact, it restored me to health when all else had failed. As I have kept well ever since I think you should know of this, that other sufferers may benefit, although my mother gave you the facts of my cure in a letter dated December 18th, 189 R."

That is the way Miss Florence Edge, of 4, Gothic Cottages, Hunter's Vale, Hockley, near Birmingham, wrote on November 9th, 1904. Her family is well and favourably known in Hockiey. "I was suffering from indigestion and debilityat the time. I got into a low weak state, always tired and heavy, and with no desire for food. Next I had pains in the pit of the stomach and sides aft3r eating, which steadily grew worse till-they were positively agonising. I grew weaker daily, and so thin and plain that I seemed to be wasting away. A hard dry coughgave nie little rest,and I suffered inuch from night sweats. Every- one who saw me thought I WAS iran declaim I was so thin and white. The least exertion caused me to faint right away. My mother carefully carried out the doctor's instructions, but in vain. His medicine did me no food.

"At last mother decided to try me with Mother Seigel's Syrup, as it had always done her good. I soon felt a difference. My appetite returned, I picked, up rapidly, the night sweats and cough soon disappeared, and steadily I gained strength, Since then I have never suffered in the same way, and I am now as well and strong as over in my life. I feel sure that this is all due to Mother Seigel's Syrup."

CHILDRTI

I should like you, in the future, to take a more active part in our "Ghildren'e Corner." I mean that I want you to write me letters with little contributions of your own for insertion in our corner. Each week I shall publish the best. I am sure many of you could send me some tales er pieces of poetry; but these must not exoeed twenty or thirty lines when printed. . _ . .

From time to time 1 shall give a prize to the best received. I don't want you to read the above and make no effort to write me anything. I am sure many of you can write me nice letters if you take just a little trouble, and there is no telling but that in the future you may be able to write stories for the magazines. The lists of new members have been coming in well. I will shortly announce the prize- winner.

Many thanks to those who have written me letters during this last week, especially to Milly Evans. I will give a prize volume to the one who first sends the best solutions to puzzles given below.

Yours affectionately, UNCLE WED.

PUZZLES FOR PLAYTIME. Easy Beheadings. 1. Behead dingles, and leaves beverages. 2. Behead to expect, and leave to attend. 3. Behead a useful instrument, and leave a tuft of hair. 4. Behead informed, and leave merchandise. 5. Behead a retinue, and leave to fall in drops. 6. Behead fanciful, and leave to distribute. 7. Behead to suppose, and leave to languish. 8. Behead at no time, and leave always. The beheaded letters will name what most children enjoy.

Riddle. Before time was I had a place, I'm vaster than created space; Yet never was my smallest part Revealed by telescopic art. Great expectations flee apace When I arrive and show my face; While hope grows brighter day by day . If luckily, I'm in the way. In empty brains I have a birth; I sum up what the spendthrift's worth; In false alarms I'm sure to be; When I pursue, "the wicked flee." For me contented minds have longed; With me the covetous feel wronged; Yet those who toil not most deserve me,— Ay, they shall win me and preserve me. C. L. M.

miners' clustered around the s' through which the ore was brought from mine.

the

Here and there a more pretentious house marked the home of a " boss" •or mining- captain. Back from the lake, rugged hills were broken by winding ravines. These hills were full of valuable minerals and beds of-roek, and covered with heavy forests. In the distance the pine-trees, over a hundred feet high, looked like tall sentinels, and the maples and birches like children just reaching to their knees. The dark green, peeping through the snow-laden branches, was a grateful break in the dazzling whiteness everywhere. "I'd like it better, if a deer were over there, and a flock of partridges, or a nest of rabbits right here," remarked Harry, as they left the village and pressed into the woods. The boys had on Indian snowshoes—long frames of hickory wood strung with deer-sinews—so they were able to walk on top of the snow without sinking through. In many places it would have been over their heads if they had sunk to the earth. Soon the ringing blows of the axe told them a lumbar camp was near. The boys passed the long, low little hut where the choppers camped. Farther on they saw the men, dressed in striped red-flannel suits, and with flannel " chucks". on their heads.

------- -------- "You've come to the wrong place for game," shouted one of the lumbermen.

---------- Bears have all gone to bed," faughed another.

"We're going to wake them up," replied Harry. .__

"They'll eat up such a little fellow as you be," was answered back. "Come, let's go the other way. Of course, they would frighten off everything near here. Hal, you are so slight! If you'd only grow out as well as up," said Tom, turning away frora the men in disgust. "I wish I'd brought a stronger boy." "That wouldn't have made deer and par- tridges come out," replied Harry, too good- natured to resent Tom's unkind remarks.

They wandered aimlessly about for some time, leaving land-marks, or rather snow-marks, so they could not lose their way. "I think a bear has set up winter quarters in that hollow tree," said Tom, at last. "Well, go and stir him up," suggested Harry. "Oh, we'll go farther on. Bears are more likely to dig a hole in the ground, or to make a house of brush. I should not wonder if there was one under that pile, there." Still Tom did not venture very near the pile of brush. "Oh, Tom, see here I—l choose this," cried Harry, who was a little in advance.

It was as pretty a baby-bear as one might wish to see. The cub seemed glad to see the boys, and gambolled around like a dog.

I wish that I were a flower, to sway / In some sweet field, where a stream was flowing: ' To have no lessons at all to say, But to watch how the white clouds floated away, And sweetened the sweet winds blowing. I'd like to sail with the breeze, and blow Through wide blue skies, where the clouds run races: To strew the orchards with summer snow,

And murmur a lullaby, soft and low, In the quiet and shady places. I think that flowers can see,—don't you? And the soft white clouds, I am sure, are playing; The wind can talk to the grasses, too, For I've listened and watched, and I'm sure they do; I almost can tell what they're saying. And when I sit in the fields, and see _

The long grass wave, when the breezes blow it, I'm just as glad as a girl eau be; And the daisies are glad, too, it seems to me, And nod their heads to show it.
