THE BOARDING-OUT OF WORKHOUSE CHILDREN.

Run corn Board of Guardians Discuss

the Question.

MR. HALLOWS' PROTEST.

The members of the Runcorn Board of Guardians at their meeting on Tuesday d,scussed the importan question of the advisability of the boarding-out 01 children under their control. Mr. A. R. Norman pi e- sided over the meeting and there were also present : Messrs. C. Roylance, H. Eyes, R. Mounfield,U.Egert, n- Warburton, J. Stokes A. Broome, T. Baxter, H. M. Davies, C. E. Linaker, J. li. Davies, J. Smith, T. Wright, H. Mawdsley, Guest, S. Woodward, Pilling, J. L. Jones, E. Bretherton, C. Horton, J. Fry( r J. Spruce S. Taylor, %V. M. Morris, P, Collins, T. F. Lea. J. W. Temperley, J. Rutter, J. Hallows, J. Whitley, R. Billington, W. Weir, T. Antwis, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), De Prez, Cook and Pearson (relieving officers). The Chairman said the question of the boarding-out of children was not a new one, having been before the Board previously. On that occasion the proposal was defeated by a small majority, but since then the circumstances and the constitution of the Board had undergone a change which he thought justified him in again bringing the matter forward In regard to the question of the boarding-out of chil- dren Board of Guardians throughout the country had been progressing and he hoped their Board would do the same. He could not but refer to toe difference which they made in the way they treated Berm' Catholic and Protestant children. He did not object to the way in which they treated the Roman Catholic children. On the contrary he had suprorted resolutions in favour of their being boarded out, but he alWays remarked that he wished they were in a position to treat Protestant children in a simile' manner. They wei e now in a position to do so and upon economical terms The total returns for the eduntry showed that 34,844 children were boarded out against 26,000 which were reared within the workhouse. In Lancashire 18 out of 20 unions dealt with the children by the boarding out system. He hoped and believed that before long there would be a Government order that they must deal with the children away from the Workhouse. His only fear was—and they had had a little experience recently— that when the order was made it would not ha on the cheapest lines. It was argued at a recent poor law con- ference that in dealing with paupers a preventative system was wanted,and one great preventative was the bringing up of children away from the taint of pauperism. Dr. llamado and the Children's Home and Orphanage were prepa ed to receive children at the rate of ss. per week when inquired into closely he believed it would he found that the cost of maintaining the children in the workhouse would be found to be very little less than that sum, but at the outside the extra cost of boarding out children would not beim ore than 6d.per head per week. It was argued that workingmen objected to the system on the ground that they had to struggle hard enough to maintain their own children but he did not believe that they took so selfish a view. Another argument against his proposal was that it would depopulate rural districts. That was not so. Many of the children from the Children's Home and Orphanage were emmigrated to Canada but the authorities were perfectly willing that they should remain in England. Dr.Barnado, how- ever, only allowed the children of decent parents to remain in thiscountry as experience had taught him that worthless parents pulled the children down to a state worsethan that from which they had been rescued. On the ground of humanity he hoped the Board would take a generons view of his motion and would not regard it altogether from the question of expense. Mr. Morris seconded. Mr. Linaker : At what age do you propose that the, children should be sent to these homes? Mt. Norman: After they have reached the age of four or five years.—ln reply to Mr. Stokes, the Chair- man said that he intended that the whole of the chil- dren in the Workhouse (35) should be affected. He was sure the adoption of the system which ho proposed would result in a very little increased expenditure to the Board. 'I he children would have to be adopted by the Board before they could be sent to homes. . . . .

Mr. Hallows said le thoroughly appreciated the spirit in which Mr. Norman had brought the matter forward A guardian ef 16 years experience was opposed to the system and informed him that at Warrington they hid decided not to hoard out children. At Wigan they boarded out children but if they had room to accom- modate them in the Workhouse they would not do so. No one had the interests of the children more at heart than he had but he must protest against the propoad made by the chairman. The Board was told that the matter was only a small one but there wero other con- siderations which increased its importance. The chil- dren in the homes were not sent out to work until they were 16 years of age whereas children in the Workhouse were sent out at the age of 14. That meant a direct loss to the Board of £3O per head per year. He intended to set his face like flint against the incurting of expense of that description as it was alsolutely unnecessary. Mr. Morris pointed out that the Board sent Roman Catholic children to homes and he thought the same privileges should be extended to Protestant children. Mr. Woodward said he was glad to think that he had taken some small part in the improving of the condition of the chi'dren under their care. He was altogether in favouriTof the idea contained in the resolu- tion, provided they could be satisfied that the cost of boarding-out the children would not entail a greater burden upon the ratepayers than they had already to bear. It was their duty to send the chil- dren away from the associations of the Workhouse to homes where they were under a high moral influence. He would like, however, to have an assurance that the permanent cost of children in homes should not be more than ss. per week, and that the cost to the Board would not be increased after two or three years had elapsed. In the boarding-out of the children they had the machinery for accomplishing the best good of tbe chil- dren in the least costly manner. Mt. fSpruce said de believed that at fthe North- Western-Poor Law Conference a suggestion would be made that there should be a combination on the part of the Boards of Guardians to deal with the question of the children, and he thought the_y should wait until they learnt what would be done. TO densely-populated districts he recognized it was desirable that the children should be sent to country homes but situated as they were did not see that there was any necessity to disturb the present arrangements. Mr. Taylpr said he dndeavoured to regard the question from a commonsense point of view, and not as a question of sentiment. The children in the Workhouse had .no opportunity of improving themselves and making provision for the futnre. They were taught no trade and at the age of 14 years were sent out as apprentices with an utter disregard to their owp inclinations as to the trade to which they were apprenticed with the result that they came back to the Workhouse. In homes they were taught a trade and became a credit to themselves. He therefore thought that in the long run it would be in the interests of economy that the children should be boarded out. Mr. Baxter objected to the proposal on the ground that it would lead to the depopulation of rural districts. He also pointed out that the schools at Dutton had been enlarged for the accommodation of children from the IWorkhouse and that the salary of the headmaster had been arrangedaccordingly. l‘fr. Woodward: If the children were taken away his salary would reduce itself automatically. Mr. Linaker moved the adjournment of the debate in order that the Board might be furnished with particulars as to the number of children who would be affected and that they might have an assurance that the cost of their maintenance In homes should be a permanent one of ss. } a week. Mr. Antxvis thought there was too much sentiment in the speeches about the workhouse taint. Ho thou,4llt that should be dropped. Mr. Stokes expressed himself in sympathy with the resolution. It was decided to adopt Mr. Linaker's motion. A motion by Mr. Col ins with regard to the boarding I out of Roman Catholic children wa also adjourned.

A NARROW ESCAPE.

A TRUE INCIDENT.

"toys, be careful with your guns," called Mrs. Brown from the door where she stood watching them out of sight. "All right, mother," they replied, laughing at her fears.

"If I can't be trusted with a gun now I'm fourteen, I'd better sell out," remarked Tom. "Well, if I'm only going on twelve, I'm as good a shot,' answered Harry. "Oh, you can shoot a chicken after it has gone to roost. I wish the Indians had left us something worth shooting," said Tom, as they climbed the hill behind their home. Here they paused, enjoying the wonderful picture before them, without realizing what gave them the pleasure of the moment. In the distance the deep blue waters of Lake Superior flashed in the sunshine. The broad, snow-covered belt of ice that skirted the water, was cleared here and there for skating, and a few children were enjoying this sport. Nowhere do children have more fun than in the Lake Superior country, for, in spite of the thermometer getting so locf- spirited, the children almost live out-of-doors, skating, coasting, or rolling down 'hill into snow-banks, as country children like to do in the hay. This village was like all mining towns. A church, school-house, and a number of small red houses, owned by the company and leased

THE "DARDANELLES."

People who wish .to be thought great travellers are often given to exaggeration. A case of this sort became painfully apparent at a table d'hôte. An illiterate man, possessing considerably more money than common sense, was expatiating upon his travels, and giving incidents of what had occurred at the various places he had visited, together with the choice and distinguished company he had mixed with. During a pause in the conversation, a gentle- man present observing lithe traveller's weak point, broke in with the query, "I presume you have seen the Dardanelles"? "Oh, yes' was the prompt reply. "Oh, yes I met the whole family sir, at Gibraltar; danced with Miss Dardanelles. Upon my word the whole family were most agreeable c'ompany—moet agreeable, I can assure you"--tableatt.

Warrington v. Leigh. Warrington inpened their season at Wilderspool on Saturday with a creditable win over Leigh,and the figure attracted a goodly crowd of quite 5,000. The weather was breezy knit fine, and the turf in perfect order. Both sides turned out at full strength, aid there was an extra cheer when Fish was seen to be in his accustomed Place, notwithstanding the reports that he would not Play for a month, Mr. F. Kenedy of Broughton acted as referee and the teams lined out as follows : Warringten—Hallam, back; Fish, Isherwood, Dicken- son, and Kenyon, three-quarter backs : Hockenhall and Brooks, half-backs ; Preston, Shug' ars Thomas,Swift, Jolley, Boardman, Belton, and Naylor, forwards.

Leigh opened in strong fashion against the breeze, and were soon menacing the Warrington line as the result of a misunderstanding between Thomas and Isher- wood. ST. HELENS LIBERALS AND THE GENERAL ELECTION.

Labour Candidate not to be Opposed.

The general committee of the St. Helens Liberal Association have considered the local position in clew of the evidently approaching general election. At a meeting, presided over by Hr. J. B. Leach, J.P., the following resolution was possed :—" That, in view of the fact that the Labour party, through the St. Helens Trades Council, have adopted and brought a candidate to contest the Parliamentary representation of the borough, the general committee of the St. Helens Liberal Association do not consider it expedient to 'nominate a Liberal candidate ; but, should any im- portant change take place in the present situation prior to the forthcoming general election, the committee call a meeting of the association and that in the meantime they take such steps as they in their judgment may ' deem necessary in the best interests of the party." A sympathetic resolution of appreciation was passed in connection with the death of Councillor J. Burchall, J.p., who, for the past forty years, had been a faithful and unfaltering leader in the great Liberal cause.

A verdict of" Death by misadventnre " was returned at West Ham, on Tuesday, on the death of Herbert Phelps Davis, a chemist, which was caused by him swallowing his false teeth after an overdose of morphia.

The Examiner, Saturday, September 9, 1905.

are both selected for the important Combination match at Oldham. Harris takes the place of Bibby. The team will be : Knight ; Short, Jordan, Marshman and Harris : Garside and Lloyd ; Swift, Coleman, Ernest Lunt, Ashton and Hewitt forwards.

Clerk had no intention of taking legal action. They had asked the farmers to abst am from encroaching upon the rights of the Corporation and it seemed to him they had no other means of defending their rights than to act upon the resolution ' of the Markets Committee. Councillors Evans and 1 Pemberton were totally wrong. The toll from the I markets was one of the few sources of making money to relieve the rates : they had a right to defend their markets. It had been stated that the market had been lin existence 40 years. That was wrong.lt first was started by bringing potatoes at a certain time to the bridge. Those not sold were sent to Manchester. They then had little opportunity of opposing, as the market was held on a public highway, and it had to do with the local authorities there to stop it. But the farmers went further and provided a field in which to hold a market. It was true the farmers were charged nothing, but it would be a wise thing for a private individual in Stockton Heath to give a field for a few years and at the end create a very successful market in opposition to the Warrington market. They owed Stockton Heath nothing. How were they injuring the farmers? He had as good a feeling for them as anyone present, but he failed to see that they were gettin„r, any favour of them by allowing certain landlords to steal the rights of the Corporation. He defended the action of the committee which was the least the council could do.

The MAYOR said it was not a question of tolls. The farmers would be charged practically nothing and would be almost on the same conditions as they were at present. The infringing of the rights of the Corpora- Ition was, however, a thing which they could not overlook.

Councillor AsHcnorr said that many people from Widnes, Wigan and St. Helens went to Stockton Heath. Where would the matter end if no steps were taken by the Corporation to defend themselves? .

CourtCalor A. PEACOCK said the committee had no illfeeling against anyone. This was simply a question of protecting their rights. The Town Clerk would not take proceedings if he could settle the question other- wise, The committee were hoping it would not be necessary to take legal proceedings. If the thing was not stopped, however, it would not end at potatoes but would result in the establishment of a market like the one at Earlestown which was within the six-mile radius.

Councillor LORD supported the amend-rne-r-tt,-an-dead he thought the action of the committee very ill-advised. When the people of Stockton Heath created a market all the year round it would be time to go to law and not until then. There had been a sale at Stockton Heath for over 50 or 60 years, and he believed the farmers had established a right to sell. Alderman TINNION said he saw no reason why they should not support the action of the committee. The rights of the committee were worth protecting, and surely the Town Clerk and the Markets Committee knew much better than any other member of the Council what was fit and proper to be done in order to protect the rights of the Corportion. Upon a vote being taken, Councillors Lord, Benson, Pemberton, Wright, iEvans,Horrocks and Sankey voted for the amendment, which was, however, defeated by a large :majority.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Now a word or two in reply to Mr. Monks. 1 must ask him for the same privilege that I requested from Mr. Macfarlane, and for the same reason, which he knows of, though Mr. Macfarlane did not. He seems very much concerned about the way in which Mr. Chamberlain's words and acts are received. There was a time when my opinion of " Joe " was as good is any one's, but he has forfeited all that. Of course, I might then have been mistaken, and I am bound to admit that subsequent events rather go to prove that I was. First there was his tinkering with the South African business,l which has never redounded, and does not to-day re- dound to our credit. I hold him more personally to blame than any man living (aye! or dead) for the prose- cution of that war, and all the evils that have since arisen out of it. I have never been able to see eye to eye with him on it, and lam dubbed with all the rest who differ from him with being a Little Englander, unpatriotic, and as one caring more for any other ' country than I do for my own. Mr. Monks claims the liberty to differ and so do I, without being called by the man I differ from foul names, and being described as one not fit to live. Then as to this Fiscal Policy. I cannot ! It is impos- sible for Tll9, and I believe this is general, to credit "Joe" with honesty for this complete change of front. The circumstances which induced him to write and say such unanswerable things in support of Free Tra4e, have not changed, and thel sayings of " Joe " to-day cannot be better refuted than by the sayings of " Joe "yester- day. in fact, I for one have lost all confidence in him, and it can never be re3tored, and if he is judged harshly he has only himself to blame. In 1900 he fought the election under false pretences, so that the Government was carried into power on a false issue. Its work has been false, and as the representatives of the people it remains false, and no effort on my part shall be wanting to upset them. But just as he raised a false issue in 1900. and everybody knows it to have been false, I firmly believe this to be false,and that it is only started now to cover up his moss and muddle in South Africa. Then with regard to patriotism. I do not yield to him or to anyone in the possession of the true sentiment, but mine is not that bastard thing which struts down the High Street of the world with its hat cocked, trailing the lappets of its coat and singing, " Will ye dare to tread on the tail of ml coat, ah ha !" It is that which advo- cates the doing of the thing which is right, not merely that which pays best. Whith believes that this Eng- of ours has a higher destiny than is conceived in a spirit of exclusiveness ; that she shall become, not merely the centre of a great Empire, and a menace to others. but the mother of free people of every clime, and that she shall flourish not by the exercise of temporal power. but by moral power, and by it become the arbiter of all international differences. I long for England to be great, but I equally long that her great- ness snail be based on right doing, that she shall be exalted, but by righteousness.—Yo urs faithfully. A. R iTHBONE, The Mount, Grappenhall. September, sth, 1905.

Mr. Macfarlane's Address Sir. —The address delivered by Mr. ThomasiMacfarlane under the auspices of the Warrington Chamber of Com- merce in the Council Chamber, on "A Canadian View of International Trade," was a most excellent one and from his point of view, no doubta most powerful one, but withal, full of worn out fallacies that have been again and again refuted, and by no one more ably than the self constituted Missionary of Protection, Joseph Chamberlain himself. I am about to take a course which may be considered unfair to Mr. Macfarlane,who at the close of his address invited the expression of an opposite opinion, or the asking of questions, which he would be glad to answer, remarking at the same time that if he was heckled, he was able to take care of himself. I hold that the Chamber of Commerce should have weight and influence in a manufacturing centre like Warrington' and lam unwilling that the views expressed by Mr. Macfarlane, should pass muster as the unchallenged expression of our Chamber, though they were not by any means as extreme as those expressed there by the advoc- ates of Protection. I regret therefore, that I was unable to respond to his invitation, through physical unfitness to enter into a. verbal dispute, and on that ground. I crave Mr Macfarlane's indulgence by allowing me to take excep- tion in this way, to some of the things which he said. Now this is a question which I agree ought to be con- sidered on its own merits in regard to its bearing on the majority of people of this country, but without regard to their social position, or if regard is to be paid to that let the preponderance of benefit go towards the poorer people. It is the fashion now-a-days to have no settled convictions, at all events on this question, and to charge the advocates of Free Trade with holding their opinions, because their fathers or grandfathers held them. Nothing could be further than this from the actual facts. We hold them because we firmly believe that by Free Trade "the greatest good is secured to the greatest number," which is surely the highest purpose of any government to legis- late for. That is our rule of conduct, and by that rule we are ready to stand or fall. It is impossible here to deal with the whole of Mr. Macfarlane's address. I will therefore take what appears to me to be its prominent features, and though there are some things which I challenge, there are others which I perfectly agree with. For instance, he said " They, that is the protectionist countries, had got to the condition of so managing things, as to cause their own people to pay for the goods which they manufacture for foreigu countries." Exactly ! That is part of our contention. Mind, I don't say Mr. Macfarlane agreed with that, but he stated it as one of the results of Pro- tection, as practised by a people who are often held up as patterns for us to imitate, and it is one of the inevit- able results of Protection. I ask therefore, if they think our people will be satisfied to know they will have to pay more for goods of home manufacture under Protection than foreigners will have to pay for the goods of the same manufacture. The people of the United States are those whom Mr. Macfarlane refers to and the amount of wages these people earn is often contrasted with our own ; but is it fair to contrast such a country with ours? I am not concerned at present with arguing that a Free Trade policy would have been better for the United States, but that Free Trade is best for us, and I do not care what form Protection takes, whether preference to our colonies or any other. If we must contrast the people, let us alsa contrast the countries. Here we have two minerals, which we win from the bowels of the earth, viz., coal and iron. One is excellent in quality, the other very doubtful, and will notanswer for many purposes, except when mixed with foreign minerals. There they have also these two minerals, both in very much greater abundance, and mach more easily obtainable than ours, for while we have to go right down into the bowels of the earth for them, there it is close to, if not on the surface, and one of them which has proved a great source of wealth to us, but a lunch greater s urce of wealth to them, is found there in a much purer state, and 'of a variety to snit present day wants better than our own. But that is not all. She, that is the United States, is blest with natural facilities of transit, and the production within her own borders, of goods of every variety and clime, which we know not of but are dependent on other nations to supply us. These goods we take in exchange for our own manufactures. It cannot be too often insisted upon, that we do not as a nation pay for goods that wereceive in money, and if we reduce the amount of purchases, we equally reduce the amount of our manufactures to pay for them. If Protection was adopted our people would be hit two blows at the smile time. One would be by the curtailment of our menu- factures due to the reduction of our purchases, result- ing in reduced earnings, and the other and more cruel blow would be due to the fact that the earnings they did get, would have less purchasing power, owing to the enhanced price of commodities, which thece taxes would cause. Ah ! but," says Mr. Macfarlane," we only want to put on a moderate tax of 2 per cent. to 5 per cent. ad valorem." Well, what then ? Does he think that the nation who he tells us make their own people pay more for the goods of their own manufacture than foreigners, will let that stand in their way ? All history proves the contrary. They would simply put up the prices to their own people to enable them to scale the wall of tariffs that had been raised against them. Mr. Macfarlane ignores that however, and assumes that an import duty of 2 per cent. ta 5 per cent, would be sufficient, and argues in effect from that, that the ex- pense for our defences would accrue to us with much 411. less trouble. In effect, that is just what the late Lord Salisbury said years ago, "That taxes were collected . "PONS VAROLI." in this way, especially on the Continent of Europe, to A doctor at a Poplar inquest stated that the deceased maintain great military systems.because the people did had died from hemorrap,e from the " pons varoli." not know what they were paying, and it galled their Juror : I didn't think it was a drowning case, susceptibilities the least." But is this desirable ?On all Coronor : It isn't. hands it is conceded that wars are too frequent. Is it .barer: What about the pond, then ? the right way to reduce this frequency, to cause The Coroner explained that the pons varoli was not a the people to find the means without knowing it? sheet of water, but an artery leading from the brain, Would it not be much better that taxes should be so and the jury seemed satisfied.

levied that everyone could not fail to know what he was paying, instead of extorting taxes by surreptitious means? On his answers to these questions will depend much of the importance to be attached to Mr. Macfarlane's recommendations. Then he reminded us that the Free Trade party adopted the principle of fighting foreign tariffs by free imports. In reply to that, I would remind him that Sir Robert Peel's Government came into power osten- sibly to uphold Protection, but that under Protection all branches of trade, especially agriculture, were and had been in such a languishing condition that commis- sions of inquiry had to be frequently resorted to, that the people were very short of work, that food was very dear, their condition, that is, thelpeople's generally, was more suggestive of a state of siege than of a nominally free people, that from beginning to end of that Parlia- ment there was one continual struggle fur mastery between the forces of Free Trade and those of Protec- tion, and that it was in the 6th year of his government that Sir Robert Peel said that he intended to fight foreign tariffs by free imports, and I appeal to the history of the last half-century. to justify the conten- tion that ir Robert Peel then held. Another thing which Mr. Macfarlane said, and to which I take exception, is used as a stock argument by the advocates of Protection in this country. He had been speaking of the maintenance of Free Imports, although other countries bad increased their own pro- j ---- daction,and invaded the markets of Free Trade England I I was not greatly enamoured of Warrington's open- with them. The men who showed the best form were Hallam, Isherwood, Hockenhall and Brooks. At half back the . local pair were very aggressive, but they showed one conspicuous fault—that of runamg among the three- • quarters. The three-quarters need to open out more, • and the halves to adopt a more forward policy. Individually they were excellent, but the combination was ineffective. On the Leigh side Johnson and Bennett were both smart, while Ciarkson invie a creditable debut at full back.

I was very pleased to learn that terms had been arranged with Fish by which he was to play against Leigh and Oldham, prior to training for his sprint match. He received a cordial welcome from the crowd and looked very fit. His pace, too has im- proved and just now I should say he was tanning faster that at any time during the last two years. The way he left such a speedy customer as Johnson proved that he will give " Hanky " Green a warm time.

In addition to the successful issue of the match. there was a very gratifying attendance, the gate money realising £BO. Mem- bers are simply rolling in and the list seems almost certain to reach the "1,000" mark this season. On Saturday Treasurer Pemberton received more in subscriptions than was taken at the turnstiles and announces 80 new members already. This is good business. The Northern Union game is a long way from being on its last legs in Warrington.

Among Saturday's games the one great surprise was the downfall of Broughton Rangers at Wheater's Field. A defeat by 23 points to 8 points requires some explaining way, and I fancy Wigan will prove a warm combination this season. Money is gradually getting a good side together, and if the c ,liters only improve on last season's form they will be among the first flight of clubs. Salford are said to be stronger than for some time past, and disposed of Hull in workmanlike fashion. Vernon Hampson and Morgan look like doing better in the red jersey than in the blue. St. Helens compiled the record score of the day 31 points to 3 against Millom. They are said to have hardly known one another so brilliant was the 'all- round display. But the Saints have stiffer hurdles to surmount yet. Runncom and 'Widnes have yet to open their programme, the 'first matches being down for to-day.

Northern Unionists will be astonished to learn that a brand-new Rugby Union Club has sprung up in their midst. As good sportsmeni feel sure they will welcome the bold intruder. Stockton Heath was years ago the home of a really-excellent Junior Rugby -Club and such men as Barber, Isherwood, Broady and Cross all came from its ranks. Since the advent of the Northern Union, however, there has been no Rugby Union football in South West Lancashire with the exception of clubs in the Manchester and Liver- pool district who have persistently upheld the amateur status. The young men of Stockton Heath have decided that there is still room for the old organisa- tion, and the new club starts off with 35 playing members. An excellent list of fixtures has been arranged as will be seen from another column, and a field off Ellesmere-road, formerly in the occupation of the Hockey Club, has been secured. I wish the new club well—there are too few signs of the spread of the Rugby game. Should the Stockton Heath Juniors unearth any talent they may recollect that the Warrington Club will always have an opening for amateurs of the right class.

One of the most popular fixtures on Warrington's card is that which takes place to-day (Saturday), when Oldham are the visitors. Ever since the formation of the Northern Union—and before—the Oldham men have been a powerful combination, and usually capable of taking the majority of points out of War- rington. Last season they won both games, though they came very near to defeat on their own ground, only a goal-kick giving them the advantage. At Wiiderspool the Warrington men made a hash of matters. Dai Davies got a kick charged down under the posts right at the start, and later on A. Lees was presented with a soft try, which brought about War- rington's downfall. There is, therefore, a debit balance against Warrington which they are anxious and determined to wipe off. With the advantage of ground and a full team to-day, I fully expect them to record a substantial win against the Watersheddings men.

There are only two changes from Warrington's team of last week, and these are in the front rank, which failed to give satisfaction: The forwards will need to play for their places this season and they might as well recognise the fact at the commencement of the season. To-day Cook and Harmer displace Swift and Belton, the full team being Hallam, full hack ; Fish. Isherwood, Dickenson and Renyon,three- qaarters; Hockeuhall and Brooks halfbacks; Preston, Boardman, Shugars, Thomas, Jolley, Naylor, Cook and Harmer forwards.

The " A " team made short work last Saturday of the Leigh juniors and ran up over 20 points at the Mather-lane. Two of the new men, Marshman (centre) and C,arside (half), made an excellent impression and

RUGBY UNION FOOTBALL CLUB FORMED.

New Ventura at Stockton Heath.

A new departure has been made in local football circles by the formation of a Rugby Union Club at Stockton Heath. Already the club claims a large number of active supporters, and an excellent fixture list has been arranged. La its officers the organization is well served, the work of an excellent committee having been ably seconded by the endeavours of the Secretary, Mr. A. F. Fawcett, Fairfield-road. Cricket.

With the coming of football, interest in cricket has completely evaporated. Warrington met Preston en Saturday, and achieved a splendid victory. The repre- sentatives of the "proud town" were all dismissed for 62, and Warrington had knocked up 09 with only four wickets down when play ceased. The chief scorer on the Warrington side was Mason, who compiled 51 and carried out his bat. He was in grand form, and his hits included four fours and eleven twos. The best bowling feat of the afternoon was that of W. H. Podmore, who captured 4 wickets at the extremely small cost of 13 runs. Mason also bowled brilliantly, accounting for four wickets for 24 runs.

The championship of the West Lancashire League was decided on Saturday, Newton by a decisive victory over St. Helens Recreation, securing the honours. The success which has attended the club's work this season is mainly owing to the brilliant play of E. M. Dowson. the Surrey cricketer, who on Saturday took seven wickets. Sankey Wire Mills sustained their second reverse of the season on Saturday when High Legh defeated them by 43 runs. For the winners Earle batted splendidly for his total of 41. None of the Wire Mills players attained double figures.

STOCKTON HEATH POTATO MARKET, The Corporation and its Rights. THIIiATENETITCIAL ACTION.

Feeling that the establishment of a potato market at Stockton Heath was an infringement of the market rights of the corporation, the Markets Committee placed the facts before Mr. F. M. Keen who advised the institution of proceedings by the Corporation for the protection of their rights. The committee there- upon resolved to authorize the Town Clerk to institute proceedings. The confirmation of the decision of the committee was moved by Councillor A. Peacock at the meeting of the Town Council which was held on Tuesday evening, and seconded by Councillor Ashcroft. Councillor EVANS thought the resolution with refer- ence to taking proceedings was rather misleading. The minute had reference to the so-termed Stockton Heath potato market—a by no means ne institution. Potatoes had been bought and sold there for a considerable time. _

The MAYOR: In the face of proceedings being recommended I think it is hardly wise for you to dis- cuss the matter. We don't want to take away any rights of the farmers and we don't want tharu to inter- fere with our market rights. Councillor EVANS : I am not going to offer any suggestion to the contrary, but this being all old institu- tion these proceedings must have been brought about by something or other. Ido notiknow what and I am not particularly anxious to know. But there is no market at Stockton Heath.

The AIANort: There is buying and selling. Is not that a market?

Councillor EVANS : I don't see that, but there is room for two opinions on the point. I think this might go back to the committee for reconsideration. I think it can be settled with legal proceedings. If we pass this resolution we scarcely know where the expense will end. Further, I don't see where they are infringing upon our rights. The MAYOR : You are treading on dangerous ground. We have our market rights which we have bought and paid for. A discussion might put us in a position we dont' want to be put in. Councillor EVANS : May I ask whether the people interested in this matter have been approached ? The Maxon: The Town Clerk says they have.

_ Councillor EVANS : How? The TOWN CLERIC : They have been approached by Mr. Place, who took the names, but said nothing about our rights etc. Councillor EVANS : For what purpose did he take their names ? Is it a question of toll ? The TOWN CLERIC : It is a question of maintaining your own rights. Councillor EVANS : Well, I think we are dealing with this matter in rather a threatening manner. I maintain methods can be adopted which will be less expensive to the town and more congenial to all con- cerned. Conciliatory measures would bring Stockton Heath and Warrington closer together and I think we can defend our rights without going to law. The Maxon: It does not follow that we will go to law. It is a question of our rights,l)v.which it is said that no other market than ours shall be established within six miles of Warrington. Councillor EYANS : But there are others. Councillor TINNION : Proceed against them. Councillor EVANS TPs, do that,and I will drop this. The MAYoR: We do not want to be unfair to the farmers._ _

Councillor EVANS: Then, what does our threatening attitude mean? Continuing, Councillor Evans, said it was a market of three nights only for five weeks in each year. That was not serious, and the market had been going on for more than 40 years. It was a slow process of taking away their rights, and why action should be taken now he did not know. The action which they proposed would create a strong feeling between those inside the borough and out. The NlAvon : They have adopted different lines. They have taken a field. They have made a place. That is the thin end of the wedge, and the Town Clark has been instructed by the committee to stop it. Councillor EVANS said the question had been sprung from some source, and some of there bad an idea where from. They knew the fashionable society of Stockton Heath did not want a market at their doors. He did not blame them for that, and means had been in.stituted to m3ve the market to somewhere else and that some- where happened to be a field which someone had offered and there was no rent to pay,. He moved as an amend- ment that the minutes relating to the market he referred back to the oommittee for further consideration.

Councillor PEMBERTON seconded, and said he agreed with all Councillor Evans had said as to the paltry amount of money the town would collect in toll. It was inadvisable for the Council to cause any friction. The farmers spent a lot of money in Warrington. The market had been held there for 40 years, and the Cor- poration They should have clai doing so led him to think there was somelstrong notice for had been very slow in claiming its rights. ?led them long ago and their not action being taken at the present time. The farmers of Cheshire contributed a great deal more to the prosperity of Warrington than many of the members of the Town Council were aware of on first tlaought. Con- sidering the paltry amount of money which the Corpor- ation would collect he thought it was ridioulous that they should cause that friction. Ha was confident that some amicable arrangement could be come to but they would not effect that by going and taking the names and addresses of the farmers. That was about the worst way the matter could possibly be approached. It was not a friendly way and was a thing which every English- man resented. Councillor MAGINNI said he believed the Town

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

The Appointment of a Senior Assistant Mistress. Sir,—May I ask the favour of your inserting,' this letter in explanation of the part I have taken in the matter of the appointment of senior assistant mistress in the Secondary School? As in my absence from the last meeting of the Education Committee my name was very freely introduced in its debate, I want to correct some of the statements then made. The simple facts regarding my action in the matter are the following. At the Schcol Management Sub-Committee meeting, whose minutes were discussed in the General Com- mittee of the 27th ult.—l repeated my former protest against the passing over of Miss Warburton in favour of one her junior both in years and in position on the staff. I also asked definitely for the reasons which led to such action. After some discussion the Director replied that "if I desired to know the real truth," (which of course was what I did desire),he would state it. This he did in terms which while not reflecting upon character,might give colourable reason for another being proposed. My reply then was that if things were as he describe', I would not prolong the controversy. But I then further enquired whether it was then proper for another lady to be passed over who was both senior in age and higher in academic position than the lady recommended? The answer was that both the previous and the present head masters preferred the present nominee to either of the others. It was then pointed out that it would not be desirable to overrido the opinions of the Head Master. This commended itself 39 probably to some extent reasonable.bnt I exoressed the wish that it should be made known that,in finally deciding to confirm the proposal, the committee was giving effect to the expressed wish of the Head Master. On being put to the vote, all the members present, except myself, voted in its favour, the Chairman(nr. A. J. Jolley) then remarking," Carried nem. con." flow it can be said, after that, that I agreed to the appointment I 9.111 at a loss clearly to understand. I did not see my way longer to vote against it, because I was not able to controvert, still less disprove, the reasons given by the Director. Being, however, by no means confident of their complete accuracy, I could not vote in favour of the proposal. I regret that Mr. Parker rather confused the matter in the remarks he made with the view of clearing it up. But I know well how far from easy it is, from memory, to represent precisely the character of any matter of the kind. May I take this opportunity of expressing my strong dissent from the view some take about the impropriety of the Education Committee traversing the action of its sob-committees? If the general body is to meet simply to confirm decisons arrived at by its sub-com- mittee, Ido not think I shall care to waste time by attending the meetings of the General Body. Of course it would be idle and irritating to question such recom- mendations unless occasion evidently required. I know only too well that the time spent in Sub-Committee work is very considerable, much more so than the public is aware of, that even the most careful Sub-Com- mittee may err in judgment, or in ignorance, and surely it is one of the functions of the general body to guard against the errors so arising. I shall certainly deem it one of my duties as a member of the Education Com- mittee to enquire about any matter upon the minutes concerning which there appears to me to be doubt and difficulty. Reverting to this especial case, let me further add that if I had supposed the question would have come to so narrow a division in voting, I should certainly have remained in town over the meeting. But being hope- less of any other than the one result, I did not deem it worth while to defer my leaving. I have, however, no interests whatever to serve except the welfare of the cause of education in the Town, and the interests of equity and fair play for those who are employed in our service. These are motives which I hope and believe are common to every member of the education authority.—Faithfully yours, Buxton, September 6th, 1905.

JOHN YONGE.

Rugby Football.

[By " Ovalist";

The Cup-holders have obtained their first points for 1905-6 in the Northern League and the South West Lancashire Cup Competition. As was the case a year ago the victims were Leigh who, however, furnished much stouter opposition this time than last. They made a determined effort to win and with a little luck and more steadiness they might hive pro- vided an unwelcome surprise at Wilderspool. Though the margin was one of seven clear points in Warring- ton's favour there was hardly that much difference between the teams and the visitors deserved a better fate than being nilled./

WARRINGTON WESLEYAN CIRCUIT.

NEW MINISTERS' ARRWAL. Sermons and Welcome Meeting.

The newly-appointed ministers to the Warrington Wesleyan Circuit (the Revs. Sidney Pitt, William Mellor, and Cecil Parker) entered upon their new duties on Sunday, when the two first named assisted in the celebration of the anniversary of Bold - street Church. In the morning the pulpit was occupied by the Rev. William Mellor who addressed a large congregation from the words, "And when they came that were hired about the eleventh hour, they received every man a penny. But when the first came they supposed that they should have received more; and they likewise received every man a penny." In concluding his remarks the preacher said that day they began the labours of another coneexional year, and he hoped that ministers and people alike would endeavour to go through it in the spirit of the teaching contained its the parable. Let them remember it was a grand thing to work for Christ. Let that spirit guide and animate through the year, and a bless- ing would come to them. Dyke's "Te Donna" was sung by the choir, Mr. Frank Hickman presiding at the organ. In the evening the Rev. S. Pitt addressed a large congregation from the words "and thou shalt love the Lard thy God with all thy heart, and with all thy soul, and with all thy mind, and with all thy strength, and thou shalt love thy neighbour as thyself," Mark xii, 30 and 31. That text contained the greatest word in the Bible. No other passage either in the New Testa- ment or the Old Testament contained the same feeling and depth of meaning as was summed up in the word love. What was love? It was a desire to possess and enjoy to sun oneself in the presence and to bask in the smile of God. To know Him and possess flim,and to live in fellowship and communion with Him. Human love passed into higher and better things and became a desire to give and bestow to spend and to be spent. The love of God passed through a similar change and elevation and became an over-mastering passion of depth and strength in order that we might fulfill His will and accomplish His purpose and blessing. The text spoke both of the love of God and the love of man but the two were so intertwined that it was impossible to treat I one without the other. Wherever the love of God was the love of our neighbour was sure to be. If anyone professed to love God and did not love his brother, his profession was a hollow sham. Could we say ,the opposite? Perhaps not with equal force, bat he believed we could say it. Wherever there was a genuine, self- sacrificing, unselfish love for humanity, there was a love of God. Love was the seat of desire, affection, passion and impulse. In other words, it was the seat of emotional life. How could we be said to love with the heart. Let them take the love of a youth for a maiden. To him cold or heat, wet or dry, sunshine or storm were as nothing when it was a question of passing one poor hour by her side. As another example of human affec- tion, take tho love of a mother for her child. That was love of the heart, and if we had not felt that love for God, we were not quite awake yet. The Psalmist felt it when he said "As the hart panteth after the water brooks, so panteth my soul after Thee, 0 God." Again we must love the Lord with all our mind. The intellect as well as the emotions must play its part in the love of God. If we truly loved Him we would use all our brains I and faculties in his service. In human love the husband studied his wife the parent his child and so in our love for God we must study him, we must bring our intellect to learn to ascertain His desire and purpose with us. That was the science of theol )gy:—the un- doubted queen of all the sciences. The business man concentrated his energy and intellect in the solving of a difficult problem with which he was confronted. How many men were there who in a similar manner brought theirintellects to bear upon religion. The love of God must have supreme place not only in the realm of intellect and emotion but also in the realm of practical activity. The late Hugh Price Hughes had been a living and a noble example of this. As a boy he gave his life to Christ and was rewarded for his self-sacrificing devotion by being given heavier burdens to bear. His death at the age of 55 was too early, but that calamity might be over-ruled if some of his spirit fell upon men and women. If a small portion were given to each one of us it v, ould be enough to make things lighter and a change would be produced which would gladden our hearts

proaut,v, gladden At Penketh the Rev. A. Cecil Parker preached. Commenting on the arrival of the new minister. "The Warrington Cir.-mit Wesleyan Methodist Church Record', says : It is only natural that the thoughts of the people will be centred upon the minister or ministers who are about to commence their new duties in their midst, and many prayers will be offered to our Heavenly Father for His blessing to rest upon them. As a people, we are apt to forget that our ministers are only human, and feel very deeply the recurring change from old friends and associations to now. But we feel sure we are only voicing the feel- ings of our people when we express the hope that the three ministers—Rev. Sidney Pitt, Rev. W. Mellor and Rev. Cecil A. Parker—who are coming amongst us, will commence their duties with the assurance of the kindly sympathy and hearty co-operation of all. And to the ministers who remain with us—Rev. Joseph Manning, Rev. John Gunnell, and Rev. J. Howard—we convey the same assurance.

WELCOME MEETING AT BOLD-STREET. - -- Rev. Joseph Manning and 'Half-Empty Churches.

An official welcome was extended to the newly- appointed ministers on Thursday when a public meeting was held in the Church. Prior to the meeting a gather- ing was held in the schools at which tea was provided. There was a large congregation in the evening Mr. J. G. Withenshaw (senior circuit steward), presid- ing. He was supported by the Revs. S. Pitt, William Mellor, J Manning, A. C. Parker,Nlessrs. J. Upson, W. Silcock, Moss, a Weaver, E. Richardson, and E. Moe ton.

The CHAIRMAN said he was pleased to note the excel- lent spirit which was spreading through the circuit. In Stockton Heath a new chapel was being erected and altogether in the whole circuit there was a very healthy tone. The circuit had been thoroughly warmed up ani he was lookmg with great expectations to the years which lay before them. That day they had entered upon a now era and extended a hearty welcome to the three gentlemen who had come to work amongst them. In Warrington they had the best ministers in the whole of the connexion, but if the work was to be a success they must all accept some amount of personal responsibility for a portion of the work. (Applause.) Mr. UPSON said he was anticipating good times in the Warrington circuit. In welcoming the new ministers he would like to tell them that they had left behind them all the spirit of criticism. The time was past for taking carping exception to what they did, and se long as they were doing God's work the Wesleyans of War- rington were determined to back them up. There was need for their labours in Warrington, as the circuit was not making that progress which they would all like to see. (Hear, hear.) The population was increasing rapidly, but their membership was not increasing. By God's grace and help they hoped to wipe off that reproach, and never rest until the work of God was abundantly prosperous in the Warrington circuit. (Applause). The Rev. JOSEPH MANNING said he thought he could promise that the kindness spoken of that night would be extended to the new ministers during their stay in the circuit. He had worked there for twelve months and could only speak well for the people of the circuit. He was quite sure they were at the beginning of better times. That was the most encouraging meeting he had ever seen in the circuit—(applause)---and he believed they were at the commencement of a year of progress 'and real success. They had before them a magnificent opportunity. He believed that meeting showed there were elements of strength in the circuit which,if rightly handled, would accomplish great things. He was con- vinced that the majority of people were ready to respond to the right chord, and he was extremely anxious that the chord should be sounded. The difficulties with which they were faced were great—perhaps greater than any outsider could tell ; but he was certain they could be overcome if only the people mot them in the spirit of unity, prayer and self sacrifice. He was anxious that they should hear the call that came to them for an effort during the winter to reach the people outside their churches. He had talked to many people during the past few months about the half-empty churches in the town and it grieved him to hear the excuses sometimes put forward. How could they excuse the half-empty churches by the statement that their people had gone out into the suburbs? What about the people who were left ? On Saturday night the streets were thronged with young people and, as far as he knew, not a single church in the town was doing anything for them on that night. The public houses were open and the theatres r trades t o li sbtue nt tthoe churchesthe eal w l of closed.etrhee multitude ie I awwshekroeedwadh°niitsniedeaabreig Christ's help in the battle of life. Taylor h Jackson... 3 s The Inspector: There is one particular item in it, Whitfield- c& b Jackson 7 and therefore, the matter comes under our cognisance. Bailey not out ...... .....01 Mr. Woodcock: The only item in this loan is Extras I The Inspector: But how much was the total expo°- . diture on Barbauld-street ? Mr. Woodcock : I cannot say that. The Town Clerk: We can find out. The Inspector: Was there any excess on the street ? The Town Clerk-: I don't think so. Barbauld-street is apart of another scheme, but I will supply you with the information you require later on. The Inspector : Will you include in future loans the cost of making up back streets? Mr. Gough : What do you mean by future loans? Is not the sum wanted included in the present applica- tion ? Vibe Inspector'replied that this application was to cover expenses that had actually been incurred in connection with the streets. There were certain transactions that had not yet been settled for the acquisition of other loans, and a further loan would be required. Mr. Gough : An approximate idea of the value of the surplus land ought to be obtained. The Inspector : I quite agree with the idea. The Inspector asked when the negotiations would be settled and the Town Clerk replied: Certainly this year. There were only two owners to deal with yet. Mr. (laugh: What I want to know is how much has been paid to each of the people concerned. The Inspector : I have had all the figures. The Town Clerk: And they have all been put in the Borough Treasurer's statement of which Mr. Gough has had a copy. Mr. Gough : But se want the figures here. The Inspector: I have had them all, I tell you. Mr. Gough: But I cannot grasp them. The Inspector: Well I have asked particularly about each one and if the public fail to grasp what is said:l cannot help it. What do you wan to know? Mr. Gough: What has been paid to Messrs. Johnson and the others. The Inspector gave the following particulars of amounts paid to owners of property :—Messrs. Johnson Bros. £575 ; Mr. T. B. Carter £4,675 ; Messrs. Greenall Whitley and Co., £1,000; Mr. James Ilepherd £1,548 ; Milner's trustees, £13,450. The purchase of Mr. J. Shaws' property was not yet settled. Mr. Gough (at a later stage) : I think the plans ought to be placed somewhere no that the ratepayers can see why the Corporation are asking for the loan before the inquiry is held. Large improvements are being carried out in the town, and yet when we come here we haven't the slightest idea ,of what they have done, or what they were asking a loan for. The Inspector; You mean you don't know what the whole scheme is going to cost. Mr. Gough : We don't know the different items that are being asked for. The Inspector : Now you know, I have given them to you. Mr. Gouah : Under the Act of 1899 they have power to spend £lO,OOO. but here is a further sum. Bow can the ratepayers judge whether they are getting value for money, unless the plans are placed before them so that they can see what is being carried out? The Inspector : You have them before you now. Mr. Hallaway: They were placed before you six years ago. The Inspector: I told you if you wanted to see them you could do so. Mr. Gough: You have all the particulars but the ratepagers are placed in a very unfair position. Tbe linspector : You can see the plans. Mr. Gough: I should be very glad if you would make that known. Mr. llallawa3 : It is all in the Act which was passed six years ago —(handing a copy of the Act to Mr. Gough). Mr. Clough: But the Act of Parliament is note plan. How can we see that the plans have been carried out according to the Act? The Inspector: Excuse me, Mr. Gough. but I have come here to hear what you or other people have to say to me, not for you to talk across the table with other people. I told you when we commenced that you could see the plans if you liked. You can see them now. Mr. Gough : I shall be very pleased to peruse them. The Inspector : Well come and look at them. Mr. Gough : I want you to see the disadvantage we are put to. Mr. E. England : It seems to me that Mr. Gough and t on this matter practically represent two schools of thought. Mr. Gough : But as ratepayers I think we are in the same Association. Mr. England: On this matter the Association has had no meeting. You are representing practically your- self and I ant doing the same. Mr. (laugh: That is rather unfair. The Inspector: Mr. England did not disturb you when you were speaking, and you must let him speak now. Mr. England said this was one of those schemes with which be thought the most thoughtful ratepayers were in hearty sympathy. They could vadily understand that no Corporation in a street widening undertaking could actually say what the expense would be. It was beyond the ken of any human being. Therefore it was not surprising that there should bosom° apparent excess It was within his own knowledge that the Corporation were put to an unnecessary expense owing to a certain opposition. Of course,there was a difference of opinion on the matter. It was decided that a certain portion of a back street should be purchased in connection with the setting back of Messrs. Hodgkinson's premises and a special scheme was brought out. Opposition was, however, forth coming. The consequence was that Id essrs- Hodgkinson's building scheme was delayed; compen-. sati on was claimed, and about .8800 were absolutely thrown away. Extra expenditure like that could not possibly have been foreseen as it was caused by a change of mina on the Dart of members of the Corporation. He preferred to look at this street improvement scheme in a broad way. Bridge-street was Warringtou's main artery and it was by that thoroughfare that people came from Cheshire into Lancashire. It was in tho interests of the whole of Warrington that the scheme should be completed at as early a date as possible. It had been going on for twenty-four years' as far as his memory I served him, and as ratepayers it did not pay them to have the work proceeded with in piecemeal fashion. He heartily supported any wise expenditure in connection with the matter. Some of the property acquired was very valuable, as it included some of the best shops in Warrington, and several public-houses. Considering these facts the excess expenditure MIS not much out of the way. As far as one could judge, the Corporation were obtaining the property at the best price possible and selling land at as high a rate as could be expected. His object was to inform the Local Government Board, through the Inspector, that many of the ratepayers felt that the sooner the scheme was completed the greater would the advantage be to the whole of Warrington. They wished to see the tramway system linked up, and

r that could not be done until Bridge-street was widened. , At present people were tempted to go shopping to Man- chester and Liverpool, but by improving the streets i they wished if possible to induceiCheshire;peopletolcome , into Warrington, and thus benefit the town. In spite of greatidifficalties. the Corporation appeared to have done all:that was really possible in the matter.! The inquiry then closed, having lasted three hours and a half. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. Fortnightly Meeting. -- The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday when there were present—Messrs. A. R. Norman (presiding), C. Roylance, H. Eyes, P. Mounfleld,G.Egerton-Warburton , J. Stokes, A. Broome, T. Baxter, H. M. Davies, C. Z. ! Linaker, J. H. Davies, J. Smith,T.Wright, 11.Mawasley, T. Guest, S. Woodward, G. Pilling, J. L. Jones, E. Bretherton, C. Horton, J. Fryer, J. Spruce, S. Taylor, W. M. Morris, P. Collins T. F. Lea, J. W. Tern perley, J. Rutter, J. Hallows, Whitley. R. Billington, W. Weir, T. Antwis, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), de Prez, Cook and Pearson (relieving officers), and Cunnah (Workhouse master). THE MAINTENANCE OF CHILDREN. The Clerk stated that the Petersfleld Guardians had forwarded the following resolution and asked for a ' similar one to be passed: "That the Local Government Board be petitioned to increase the limit of payment allowed for children boards I out with foster parents from 4s. to 6e. per week at the discretion of the Guardians." The letter was allowed to lie on the table. THE TREATMENT OF VAGRANTS. The delegates who attended a conference of Guardians at Lancaster on Friday reported that resolutions were passed in favour of the State providing farm and labour colonies for the more or less detention of habitual tramps, against the bestowal of indiscriminate alms by the public and in favour of Guardians providing casuals with a mid-day meal before they left the wards. Boards of Guardians in the North of England were asked to appoint representatives to serve on an inter-county committee for thepurpose of devising the best means for carrying the resolutions into effect. Mr. Stokes thought something should be done to stop the continued increase in the number o.? professional vagrants. Mr. Antwis : It is nothing short Of a disgrace to allow them to be on the roads. They should be liable to arrest if they are round on the roads. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Wright was appointed delegate to attend the Conference.
